A black hole is a local one-way membrane in a larger loaf, made when a clump loses an argument with gravity. A white hole is that film run backward: a local fountain no one has seen. The bang was not local. There was no outside to explode into, no warehouse, no shrapnel crossing a pre-existing dark. Chapter 22 will give the white hole its four rooms and then take the analogy away. Here: **a hole is a place. The bang was a time.** You can write math in which a collapse, time-reversed, looks like a bang. You cannot point at the leftover glow and say “that was a star that burst.” Stars come later. Holes come later. The fog comes first.

How old, if we are allowed a kitchen number? About 13.8 billion years, give or take a hundred million, counted from the hot fog we can reconstruct — not counted from a waiting room labeled BEFORE. That is a hot-enough claim for this chapter: the leftover glow, the leftover helium, and the stretch-rate of now, run backward through the equations, meet at a finite age. The number will get a few more digits later. It will not turn into infinity.

Then why is the part we can see about 93 billion light-years across, if light has only had 13.8 billion years to travel? Because the dough kept rising while the light was in transit. A photon that has been flying for 13.8 billion years did not fly through a frozen warehouse. The places it left have been pulled farther apart the whole time. The farthest stuff whose light has had time to reach us sits, today, at a proper distance of about 46 billion light-years. Diameter, roughly 93 billion. That is not the universe’s edge. It is our archive’s edge. Chapter 26 will make the distinction official. Here: the loaf is larger than the slice we can still taste.

So what was it *from*?

Not from a kitchen nothing — not from an empty room with the lights off and a magician. Empty, in physics, is already a something: a vacuum with rules, jitters, a leftover shove, a geometry. Pair production makes a particle and its opposite from energy, and the energy had to be in the account. The early soup made particles and antiparticles by the truckload and then almost canceled them. We are the rounding error: about one leftover nucleon per billion photons, Chapter 10’s ratio, the reason atoms are a footnote. That leftover is a *warm* problem with a name, baryogenesis — why the cancellation was rude by one part in a billion. We have recipes (a bit of leftover difference between matter and antimatter, a bit of out-of-equilibrium cooking). We do not have the dish photographed. “Matter from nothing” is a poster. Matter from a slightly unfair soup is the grown-up line.

Andrei Sakharov, in 1967, wrote down what a kitchen would need to keep one extra ladle of matter when the soup tried to cancel. Three conditions, in ordinary language. First, the books must be allowed to go out of balance: the soup must be able to change the baryon count, to make a nucleon without making its twin. Second, the soup must be rude in a particular way. It must treat matter and antimatter a little differently, and it must treat left and right a little differently, which physicists write as C violation and CP violation — charge conjugation and its partner, the combined swap of particle-identity and mirror. Third, the cooking cannot be in equilibrium. A pot that has fully settled forgets its rudeness; the extra ladle gets undone on the way back. You need a moment when the reactions cannot keep up with the cooling — a slammed oven door, a stretch that outruns the chemistry.

Those three are warm. We have seen C and CP rudeness in particle kitchens on Earth, in kaons and B mesons, at the level of a small insult, not yet at the level of one leftover in a billion. We have not photographed the cosmic moment. The ratio is hot. The recipe that produced the ratio is a research program. Do not let a poster cash the check.

What about the soup’s own origin — the first slice of loaf?

Inflation, if you buy it, pushes the question back: a burst of stretch, then a reheating that *is* the hot fog. The burst still wants a field, a vacuum, a reason. Quantum-gravity sketches try to stop using “before”: Hartle and Hawking round the loaf off so time only becomes time when there is enough of it; Vilenkin tunnels from a technical nothing that is not a kitchen nothing. Those are cold, and they are allowed to be. The fossils do not reach that room. They reach the fog, the three minutes, the glow. **We do not owe you a waiting room labeled BEFORE in order to say the fog was real.**

For now, retire the grenade. Retire the bursting hole. Keep the dough. The past we can rebuild is not a point. It is a hotter, smaller-spaced version of everywhere, made of a soup that almost canceled and didn’t, and we do not yet own the first word of the recipe. We own the leftovers. That is already a courtroom.

---

\newpage

## 8. Everything Is Stretching

In the 1910s, Vesto Slipher, working nights at a stubborn telescope in Arizona, noticed that the light from many spiral nebulae was shifted toward the red. Red, in the physics of waves, is the stretch. A siren falling away from you drops in pitch for the same family of reasons. Slipher did not yet know those nebulae were other galaxies. He had the stretch before he had the nouns.

The years were 1912 to 1917, more or less, a run of plates at Lowell Observatory. Slipher was measuring spectra: the fingerprints of atoms in light — hydrogen, calcium, the usual kitchen of lines. In spiral nebulae those lines sat in the wrong place, shifted toward the red for most of them, toward the blue for a few. Receding, approaching. He did not yet have a distance ladder tall enough to turn the shifts into a law. He had a pile of redshifts and a puzzle. By 1917 he had two dozen spirals, and the pile was mostly red. The sky was already voting.

A law needs a ruler, not just a pile. The first honest cosmic ruler was a kind of star that brightens and dims on a schedule. Henrietta Leavitt, in 1912, working through plates of the Small Magellanic Cloud at Harvard, found that the longer the period, the brighter the star. Cepheids, named for the prototype in Cepheus. Once you have that relation, a Cepheid is a standard lamp: measure how fast it winks, infer how bright it truly is, compare with how faint it looks, and you have a distance. Hubble used those lamps to show that the spirals sat far outside the Milky Way. Then he used them again as rungs. Without Leavitt there is no Hubble law. There is only Slipher’s pile, glowing and unnamed.

Hubble and Milton Humason turned the stretch into a law. Plot distance against recession speed and you get a line, sloppy at first, cleaner every decade since:

*v = H_0 × d*

*where v is how fast a distant galaxy recedes, d is its distance, and H_0 — Hubble’s constant — is the slope, the stretch-rate of now.*

Humason had been a mule driver on the mountain before he became the person who could hold a faint galaxy on a slit for hours. The 1929 paper was a handful of points. The 1930s were Humason going fainter, the line getting longer, the slope refusing to go away. Hubble’s first number was about 500 kilometers per second per megaparsec, wildly high, because the distances were wildly short. The modern fight is not whether there is a slope. The modern fight is whether the slope is nearer 67 or nearer 73.

The number has been fought over for a century and is still being fought over a little. The fight is about the second digit, not about the existence of the line. Galaxies recede. Farther means faster. That is hot.

Say the digits out loud, because the argument is real and it is not a crack in the bang. One camp reads the leftover glow of Chapter 9 through the simple expanding model and gets about 67 kilometers per second per megaparsec. Another camp climbs Leavitt’s ladder in the nearby universe — Cepheids in hosts that also explode in a particular way — and gets about 73. Both error bars are small enough that the two numbers do not overlap. That is the Hubble tension. It may mean a rung is still mis-cut. It may mean the simple model is missing a late-time ingredient. It does not mean the raisins have stopped receding. Appendix A8 keeps the algebra. Here: the first digit is a 6 or a 7. The existence of the stretch is not on the table.

The later ruler, the one that extends the ladder past where Cepheids fade, is a kind of exploding star: Type Ia, a white dwarf that goes thermonuclear with a peak brightness you can standardize. Chapter 18 will hire those explosions to show that the stretch is speeding up. Do not steal 1998 from that chapter. This chapter only needs the job description. Cepheids get you to nearby galaxies. Type Ia get you farther. Both are lamps. Both can be wrong in the second digit. Neither is optional if you want a number for *H_0* that is not just a slope on a chalkboard.

A megaparsec is a terrible kitchen length and a perfect cosmic one: about 3.26 million light-years, the distance at which the Hubble law starts to drown out the local shoving. Nearby, galaxies have peculiar velocities — they fall toward neighbors, swing in groups, approach like Andromeda, which is coming toward us at about 110 kilometers per second because we already touched. The Hubble law is not a traffic ticket issued to every smudge. It is the motion that remains after you leave the bound clump. Plot only the Local Group and you will not find a slope. Plot past a few tens of megaparsecs and the slope sits up. *H_0* in kilometers per second per megaparsec is a kitchen invoice: so many extra kilometers per second of recession for each extra megaparsec of distance. At 70, a galaxy 100 megaparsecs away recedes at about 7,000 kilometers per second. That is the stretch-rate of now as a cash register.

The redshift itself is not a guess. It is a shift of named lines. Hydrogen’s Lyman-α, the calcium H and K pair Slipher knew, the oxygen lines that light up star-forming gas: you know where they sit in a rest-frame kitchen, because you can measure them in a lamp. In a distant galaxy they sit redward by the same factor for every line. That common factor is *1 + z*. If the lines disagreed with one another, the stretch would be a fraud. They do not disagree. A single *z* slides the whole fingerprint. A speedometer reading of *v = cz* is only a translation for small *z*. At *z = 1* you are not allowed that translation. You are allowed the scale factor.

What is stretching is not the galaxies themselves, as if someone had put Andromeda on a rack. Bound things — people, planets, solar systems, galaxies — hold together. What stretches is the space between the unbound. Light traveling through that stretch gets its wavelength pulled longer. A photon that left a hydrogen atom as a sharp ultraviolet line arrives here as a tired red one. The amount of stretch is written *1 + z*, where *z* is the redshift. A galaxy at *z = 1* sent its light when the universe was half the scale it is today. A galaxy at *z = 6* is a teenager from when the first big galaxies were still assembling. The leftover glow of Chapter 9 sits at *z* about 1,100. We are looking at the universe when it was about a thousandth of its present size.

Put the scale factor back on the table, because the sentence deserves its own line:

*a = 1 / (1 + z)*

*where a is how large unbound distances were when the light left, compared with today, and z is how much the wavelength has been pulled.*

At *z = 1*, *a = 1/2*: half-size. At *z ≈ 1,100*, *a ≈ 1/1,100*: the leftover glow. Redshift is not a speedometer glued to a galaxy. It is a receipt for how much the dough rose while the photon was in the mail.

Nadia keeps a sourdough starter and a notebook of doubling times. Every so often the recipe doubles: twice the flour, twice the water, twice the bowl. The raisins that have already touched — the ones that stuck into a clump while the dough was still shy — stay a family. They do not peel apart when the next doubling comes, because they are bound: gluten, sugar, a crust. The raisins that never touched keep receding, invoice after invoice, each doubling a larger kitchen. That is the Hubble law as a baker’s account. You, Earth, the Solar System, the Milky Way: a family that already touched. The next cluster over, if it is not bound to us, is a raisin on a later invoice. Bound systems do not stretch. The space between the unbound does. Your meter stick does not grow overnight. Andromeda is not on a rack. The gap between here and a galaxy at *z = 1* is.

![Figure 8. A handful of distant galaxies, cropped so each smudge stays sharp. Almost every one is receding.](Figures/figs/fig08.jpg)

There is a cruelty hidden in the kindness of the deep field. The most interesting smudges are so far that their light has been in transit for most of the age of the universe. We will never send a reply that arrives while anything there still resembles what we photographed. Chapter 26 will make that official. Here it is enough to feel the stretch in your hands: the night sky is not a room. It is a history of rooms, stacked by how much they have been pulled since the light left.

The James Webb Space Telescope did not invent high redshift. It made it rude. There are galaxies at *z > 10*. That sentence is hot: spectroscopically confirmed objects from when the universe was a few hundred million years old, already shining as galaxies, not as a rumor of gas. The fight — and it is a real fight — is how mature they look. Some of them seem too massive, too dusty, too done for the time on the clock, if you feed certain models of how fast stars should have assembled. That fight is warm. It is a fight about the first factories, not about whether the dough rose. Early galaxies exist. How quickly they put on weight is a measurement in progress, not a loophole in Chapters 9 and 10.

There is a distinction the newspapers keep flattening. Light-travel time is how long the photon was in the mail. Proper distance now is where the sender sits in today’s dough. At *z = 1* the mail took about eight billion years; the sender now sits farther than eight billion light-years because the dough rose in transit. At the leftover glow the mail took 13.8 billion years and the sender’s current seat is about 46 billion light-years. Chapter 7 already gave you the diameter. This chapter only needs you not to treat *z* as a speed and not to treat lookback time as a ruler you can lay on the table without the scale factor.

Humason’s faint galaxies were at redshifts we would now call modest. JWST’s spectroscopic *z > 10* objects are not modest. The measurement is a set of lines, not a vibe. When a break or an emission line sits where *1 + z > 11* says it should, you are not looking at a nearby dwarf with bad luck. You are looking at a factory from the first few hundred million years. How chemically grown-up that factory already was — how much dust, how many stars, how settled a disk — is the warm argument. That it existed is not.

One more detail, because it is beautiful and true. The Hubble law does not pick a center. If every raisin recedes from every other raisin in proportion to distance, then *your* raisin is allowed to write the same law. The slope is a property of the dough, not of a privileged kitchen.

A rough inverse of *H_0* is a Hubble time. If the slope is 70 kilometers per second per megaparsec, *1/H_0* is about 14 billion years. That is not the age, because the stretch-rate has not been constant — matter used to brake, the shove now speeds — but it is the same order as 13.8, which is why a slope could smell an age before the leftover glow gave us a clock. Hubble’s first number implied something like two billion years and was already in a fight with rocks. The second-digit fight of 67 versus 73 is a fight inside a number that finally agrees with the stones.

Appendix A8 writes the redshift into the scale factor and shows how *H_0* is a snapshot of a function *H(t)* that has not been constant. Dark energy, when we get there, is a statement about that function’s later life. The stretch is older than the speeding-up. The speeding-up is a plot twist, not the opening scene.

---

\newpage

## 9. A Baby Picture in Microwaves

In 1964, Arno Penzias and Robert Wilson were trying to make a radio antenna in Holmdel, New Jersey, shut up. It hissed. The hiss came from every direction. They cleaned out the pigeons. They checked the wiring. The hiss stayed. A few miles and a few conversations away, a Princeton group was looking for exactly that hiss: the leftover heat of a once-hot universe, stretched by expansion into microwaves, cooled to a few degrees above absolute zero.

The Princeton group had names, and they had a theory, and they were a little late. Robert Dicke had built the kind of radiometer that can hear a sky this quiet. Jim Peebles had calculated that a hot, dense past should leave a glow at a few degrees Kelvin. Peter Roll and David Wilkinson were getting ready to look. In 1965 the Holmdel hiss and the Princeton calculation met, and the leftover glow stopped being a prediction. It became a measurement. Penzias and Wilson had not been hunting a bang. They had been hunting a clean antenna. The universe does not care what you were hunting.

They had found the baby picture.

It is not a photograph in the camera sense. You cannot walk outside and see it. You need a radio ear. But it is as real as the Sun, and in some ways ruder, because it is in your kitchen too. A small fraction of the snow on an old analog television was this glow, leaking into the living room, older than any continent.

Tomas kept a set in the basement after the stations went digital, out of stubbornness and because the hiss was company. A few percent of that snow, if the set is analog and the antenna is honest, is not dead channels. It is 13.8 billion-year-old light, stretched into microwaves, leaking through the walls. You can stop watching. You cannot make the glow go away. It is in the basement, in the attic, in the space between the casserole and the ceiling. That is what “everywhere” sounds like when it has cooled.

Why microwaves, not visible light? Because the oven cooled. A blackbody’s peak wavelength stretches with *a*. At last scattering the fog was optical: 3,000 K, a dull red if you could have had eyes, which you could not. Stretch by a thousand and the peak sits near a millimeter, in the microwave, a frequency around 160 gigahertz on the FIRAS curve. That is why Penzias and Wilson heard a hiss on a radio horn and not a glow on a photographic plate. Alpher and Herman had predicted a leftover of a few degrees Kelvin in 1948. They did not have Holmdel. They had the right order of magnitude and a number that waited seventeen years for an antenna that would not shut up.

The temperature is 2.725 degrees Kelvin — two and three-quarters degrees above the coldest anything can be. The spectrum is a blackbody so clean it makes laboratory blackbodies look sloppy. A blackbody is the glow of something in thermal equilibrium, a “we have mixed thoroughly” signature. The early universe was mixed. Then it stretched, and the glow stretched with it, and the mix was frozen into the sky.

COBE, the Cosmic Background Explorer, carried the instrument that made that sentence unkind to rivals. FIRAS — a spectrophotometer built to argue with the sky — found a blackbody at 2.725 K so tight that the error bars sit inside a line on a plot. John Mather’s team had measured the temperature of the universe the way a baker measures an oven: not by mood, by a curve. If you want a hot claim with a decimal, take that one. The leftover glow is not “about three degrees.” It is 2.725, and it is a blackbody, and the departures from that curve are so small they had to be hunted on purpose.

A blackbody this clean is also a veto. Starlight bounced off dust, a fog of leftover starlight, a glow from nearby radio junk: those stories produce the wrong shape. They can make a hiss. They cannot make FIRAS. The curve is the courtroom. The pigeons were a reasonable first suspect. They were not a spectrum.

![Figure 9. The microwave sky as a gray oval. Warmer and cooler patches are labeled in words. This is a measurement, not a snapshot from a camera.](Figures/figs/fig09.jpg)

At first the picture looked boring: the same temperature everywhere, to a part in a thousand. Then to a part in a hundred thousand. COBE, in the early 1990s, found the freckles — slightly warmer, slightly cooler patches. WMAP and Planck mapped them until they looked like a weather chart for a day 380,000 years after the beginning of the stretch.

The freckles have a date. COBE’s DMR map, in 1992, showed that the sky is not perfectly bland: differences of about one part in 100,000, the first photograph of the seeds Chapter 14 will grow into galaxies. George Smoot’s team had not found continents. They had found the rumple in the dough. WMAP, through the 2000s, turned the rumple into a spectrum of sizes. Planck, with maps in 2013 and a final cosmology release in 2018, sharpened the weather chart until the peaks in that spectrum became a courtroom of their own: how much ordinary matter, how much extra pull, how fast the stretch, how flat the large-scale geometry. Those later maps are how Chapter 11 gets its percentages. This chapter only needs the baby: a blackbody with freckles, photographed on the whole sky.

Why 380,000 years? Before that, the universe was a plasma: nuclei and electrons, loose, swimming in light. Light could not travel far. It kept bouncing off free electrons, the way sunlight has trouble getting out of the middle of the Sun. When the universe cooled enough for electrons to lock onto nuclei and make the first hydrogen atoms, the fog lifted. Photons that last scattered then have been flying in straight-ish lines ever since. We photograph the surface of last scattering the way you photograph a cloud bank from an airplane: you do not see through it. You see the top.

Two nouns get mixed here, and they should not. *Recombination* is the chemistry: electrons locking onto nuclei, the universe becoming electrically neutral for the first time. (The name is a fossil; nothing had combined before. It was combination. The word stuck anyway.) *Last scattering* is the optics: the last time a typical photon bounced off a free electron and then ran. They happen at nearly the same age, because once the electrons are locked, the fog has nothing to bounce off. Nearly is not identical. A few electrons stay free. The last bounce is a little later than the locking, a surface with a thickness, not a pane of glass. Kitchen language: the soup sets, and then the last steam stops hitting the lid. We photograph the lid.

Before the fog lifted, a photon’s mean free path was a kitchen, not a cosmos: bounce, bounce, bounce, like light in milk. After, the mean free path became the size of the observable universe. That is the whole optical trick. We are not looking through milk. We are looking at the last skin of milk, stretched into an oval.

The temperature then was about 3,000 K — a red-hot fog, the color of a stove element, not a microwave. Stretch the wavelengths by *1 + z ≈ 1,100* and 3,000 K becomes 2.725 K. The number is not a coincidence. Temperature of free light scales with *1/a*. Cool the oven by stretching the kitchen.

One large pattern is not a freckle and should not be sold as one. The whole sky is a few thousandths of a degree warmer in one direction and cooler in the opposite direction: a dipole. It corresponds to a motion of about 370 kilometers per second toward Leo, the Sun’s peculiar velocity relative to the leftover glow. We are not at rest in the dough. We orbit the Galaxy; the Galaxy falls toward a crowd; the crowd has a velocity of its own. Maps like Figure 9 have that dipole subtracted, the way you subtract the motion of the train before you argue about the scenery. What remains is the baby picture, not our weather.

The oval is not raw sky. Our own Galaxy shines in microwaves too — dust, electrons spiraling in magnetic fields, a plane of junk across the middle. Planck’s maps are the sky after that junk has been modeled and taken off, a subtraction as necessary as wiping flour off a scale. What you are allowed to call cosmology is the remainder. WMAP, the Wilkinson Microwave Anisotropy Probe, carries David Wilkinson’s name because he was one of the people waiting in 1965 and one of the people who spent a career making the wait into a map. He died before the full harvest. The probe did not. The sky does not pause for obituaries.

Inside the freckles there is a sound, almost literally: the plasma, before last scattering, could ring. Overdense patches compressed, bounced, compressed again, a fluid with light as the spring. Those acoustic oscillations froze at 380,000 years into a preferred set of angular sizes on the sky — peaks in a power spectrum. Planck’s 2013 and 2018 papers are, in large part, a reading of those peaks. This chapter will not steal Chapter 14’s seeds or Chapter 11’s inventory. It will say that the baby picture is not a blur. It is a freeze-frame of a ringing fog, and the ring has been measured.

Those freckles are not decoration. They are the seeds of Chapter 14, written in temperature. A patch that was a little denser then is a little more likely to be a cluster of galaxies now. The baby picture is also a will.

The evenness of the picture is its own unpaid bill. Look at two patches on opposite sides of Figure 9. Their temperatures match to a part in a hundred thousand. In the simple bang of this part, those patches have never been in each other’s past light cones. They have not had time to talk, to mix, to agree. That is the horizon problem. It is a real problem. It is not a reason to throw out the leftover glow. It is a pointer: Part III exists because this sky is too even for a kitchen that never shared a pot. Do not solve it here. Notice that the baby picture both confirms the hot fog and hands you a puzzle the fog, by itself, does not pay.

Hot claim: the leftover glow exists, is a blackbody, is almost the same in every direction, and has freckles at the level of one part in a hundred thousand. Warm claim: those freckles are the fingerprint of slightly uneven density in a hot, expanding plasma. Cold claim: any story of the first 10⁻³² seconds that does not eventually produce this picture is entertainment.

When you look at Figure 9, you are not looking at a place. You are looking at a time. The oval is the entire sky. The entire sky is one age. That is what “the explosion happened everywhere” looks like when it has had 13.8 billion years to cool in public.

---

\newpage

## 10. The First Three Minutes

Steven Weinberg gave this interval its fame, and he was right to. Between about a hundredth of a second and a few minutes, the universe did something we can reconstruct with the same nuclear physics we use in the Sun and in weapons labs. Before that, the story gets warmer and then cold. After that, for a long time, not much of nuclear interest happens. The three minutes are a workshop. We still own the tools.

He was not the first to guess that a hot past would cook light nuclei. In 1948 Ralph Alpher and George Gamow published a paper whose third author was Hans Bethe, added as a joke so the names would read α, β, γ — alpha, beta, gamma, the start of a Greek kitchen. The αβγ paper argued that the early universe could build nuclei by capturing neutrons, and that a leftover glow should still be around. The capture-chain details were wrong in ways that later work had to repair: you cannot climb the whole periodic table in three minutes, because there are no stable lodgings at five or eight nucleons. The helium insight was the seed. The leftover-glow insight was the other seed. Weinberg’s book, decades later, is the workshop with the tools calibrated. Gamow’s joke is how the idea first got a table.

At a hundredth of a second, the temperature is about a hundred billion degrees. No atoms. No nuclei that last. A soup of light, electrons, positrons, neutrinos, and a few leftover nucleons — protons and neutrons — swimming in a glare that would unmake any chemistry you have a word for. The nucleons are outnumbered by photons about a billion to one. That ratio is still written in the sky. It is why the universe is so empty of atoms compared with light, and why we are a footnote in a photon’s memoir. The billion dead twins are the antimatter that did not get a chair. Food, when we get to it, is a later argument about leftovers. The first leftover is this: the soup was unfair, and the unfairness is why there is anyone to eat.

Write the temperature once, as a kitchen clock, so the later ticks have a place to hang.

*T ≈ 10¹¹ K at t ≈ 0.01 s*

*where T is the temperature of the soup and t is time since the hot fog we can reconstruct — not since a waiting room.*

At that glare, electrons and positrons are still being born in pairs from photons and annihilating back. Neutrinos still talk to the rest of the pot. The nucleons are a trace ingredient, a pinch of salt in a vat of light.

Neutrons are slightly heavier than protons. In equilibrium, there are fewer of them. As the soup cools, the reactions that turn protons into neutrons and back slow down. At about one second, neutrinos stop talking to the rest of the soup — they *decouple* — and the neutron-to-proton ratio freezes near one-to-six, then keeps dropping slowly because free neutrons decay. You have about ten minutes before most of the leftover neutrons would be gone. The universe does not give you ten minutes of workshop time. It gives you about three.

The free neutron’s lifetime is about fifteen minutes. That is why the workshop is in a hurry. Leave the nucleons sitting as a warm gas with no oven and the neutrons wander off into protons, electrons, and ghostly leftovers, and there is nothing left to glue into helium. Ten minutes would have wasted most of the inventory. Three minutes spends it. The clock is nuclear, not rhetorical. A kitchen timer on the counter is the same kind of object as this: a half-life with consequences for the recipe.

Decoupling, here, is not a mood. It is a race. The soup is thinning and cooling. The weak reactions that let neutrinos knock nucleons around get slower. When the reaction rate falls behind the stretch rate, the neutrinos leave the conversation, streaming as their own leftover — a cosmic neutrino background we have not photographed as a map, but whose presence is felt in the helium and in the leftover glow’s peaks. One second is the door slam. The neutron fraction at the slam, plus a little decay on the walk to minute three, is the inventory the workshop will be allowed to spend.

At roughly three minutes, the temperature has fallen to a billion degrees. Deuterium — a proton glued to a neutron — can at last stay stuck. Before that, the glare broke deuterium as fast as it formed. This is the deuterium bottleneck, a traffic jam with a clock. Once deuterium survives, helium-4 assembles with almost greedy efficiency. There are no stable nuclei with five or eight nucleons, so the assembly line stalls at helium. A sprinkle of lithium is a leftover rounding error. Almost every neutron that survived is now locked in helium.

*T ≈ 10⁹ K at t ≈ 3 min*

*where that billion-degree tick is the oven setting at which deuterium stops being smashed, and helium can be baked.*

If nearly every leftover neutron is locked in helium-4, and you started with about one neutron per seven nucleons after decay, the mass fraction of helium sits near one quarter.

*Y_p ≈ 0.25*

*where Y_p is the primordial mass fraction of helium-4, counted before stars had a chance to lie.*

Deuterium that escaped the greedy step survives as a sprinkle.

*D/H ≈ 2.5 × 10⁻⁵*

*where D/H is deuterium nuclei per hydrogen nucleus in uncooked gas, a thermostat for how crowded the nucleons were in the pot.*

![Figure 10. Four huge ticks on a single line: 1 second, 3 minutes, 380,000 years, now. Temperature lives under each tick.](Figures/figs/fig10.png)

Then the workshop closes. The soup is still too hot for atoms. It will be too hot for 380,000 years. But the nuclear work is done. The universe has committed, by weight, to about three-quarters hydrogen and one-quarter helium, plus a dust of the rest. Stars will later lie about this. They will burn hydrogen into more helium and brag. The *first* helium is not their brag. It is a fossil.

Weinberg’s afterword, years later, had to make room for inflation and for freckles in the leftover glow. He did not have to take back the three minutes. The three minutes have only gotten sharper. That is what a hot claim feels like when you meet one in the wild.

If you could inventory the ordinary atoms in the universe — not the dark extra pull, not the dark extra shove, the atoms — you would find that about one-quarter of their mass is helium-4. That is an absurd amount of helium to blame on stars.

Stars are helium factories, yes. Our Sun is making helium in its core right now, at a stately, suburban pace. In ten billion years it will have converted a respectable fraction of its hydrogen. It will not have converted a quarter of the hydrogen in the *universe*. There has not been time, and there have not been enough stars. The first stars, which were massive and brief and sloppy, made a mess of carbon and oxygen and the iron in your blood. They did not flood intergalactic space with a quarter-by-mass of helium. Something else did.

Say that as a budget, not as an insult. Take every star that has ever shone in the observable loaf, give it a generous lifetime, let it burn at a generous efficiency, and you still cannot cook a quarter of all baryons into helium. Stars make *metals* — astronomers’ rude word for anything heavier than helium — in interesting amounts. They make extra helium locally, in cores and in winds. They do not set *Y_p*. The sky’s baseline helium is older than the first star. That is why the workshop is not optional, and why Alpher and Gamow, joke author included, were pointing at the right oven even when the recipe was still a mess.

The something else is the workshop, caught in the act.

Astronomers measure primordial helium in the least sophisticated gas they can find: dwarf galaxies that have made almost no stars, clouds that have not had a chance to lie. The number they keep finding sits near a quarter by mass, with a little scatter and a lot of argument about the second digit. Deuterium, which stars destroy more easily than they make, is a second fossil. Too much deuterium and the early workshop was too empty of nucleons. Too little and it was too crowded. The observed sprinkle of deuterium is a thermostat for the nuclear minutes. Lithium is the sulky cousin: the prediction and the measurement still do not quite shake hands. That fight is real and unfinished. It does not cancel the helium.

The practical kitchen for helium is an H II region: a nebula where hot stars have stripped electrons off hydrogen, so the gas glows in lines you can put a spectrometer on. Look in metal-poor dwarf irregulars — galaxies that have barely cooked a periodic table — and the helium lines sit near that quarter by mass. Look at deuterium in high-redshift clouds, uncooked by stars, and you get a few deuterium nuclei per hundred thousand hydrogen nuclei, about *2.5 × 10⁻⁵*. The photon-to-baryon ratio that makes those numbers come out is the same billion-to-one already sitting in the leftover glow: about *10⁹* photons per nucleon. Two fossils, one crowding number. Lithium-7 is the exception that still itches: the workshop predicts two to three times more than the oldest stars seem to hold, or the stars seem to hold two to three times less than the workshop baked. Factor two or three, not a factor of a hundred. Warm, unfinished, not a loophole.

The lithium fight has a name on the stellar side: the Spite plateau, a floor of lithium-7 in the oldest, most metal-poor stars, lower than the three-minute prediction by a factor of two or three. Either the stars ate some after they formed, or the workshop’s lithium-7 channel is still missing a correction, or both. People who work on stellar atmospheres and people who work on nuclear rates have been polite at each other for years. The helium does not care. The deuterium does not care. Lithium is allowed to be the sulky cousin and still sit at the table.

Here is the detail that makes the fossil personal. Every breath you take is mostly nitrogen and oxygen, cooked in stars that died before the Sun was a cloud. The helium in a party balloon is mostly not that. Some of it leaked from Earth’s crust, where uranium and thorium have been throwing helium nuclei like litter for eons. Some of the helium in the *cosmos*, the quarter that is not a party trick, has been helium since before there was a periodic table with more than two honest entries. You cannot hold that helium. You can know it.

Ruth once tied twenty balloons to a porch rail for a birthday and asked, as a joke, how much of the universe she was holding. Almost none, and the wrong helium besides. Party helium is a crustal leak, a local mining of radioactive rock. Cosmic helium is the workshop’s payroll, locked at minute three, mixed into every uncooked cloud. The porch rail is not a detector. The metal-poor dwarfs are.

Hot claim: the light-element abundances match a hot, expanding workshop with a known nucleon-to-photon ratio. Warm claim: the remaining lithium tension is a problem for astronomers and nuclear theorists, not a loophole for a cold beginning. Cold claim: any rival origin story that cannot produce this helium is not an origin story. It is a mood.

The leftover glow says the universe was once a mixed, hot fog. The leftover helium says the fog did nuclear work on a three-minute clock. Two fossils, two methods, one past. That is as close as cosmology gets to a courtroom.

---

\newpage

## 11. Almost Nothing, Almost Everywhere

Smooth the universe. Take every star, every planet, every wandering rock, every atom in every lung, and spread them until the lumps are gone. You will have, on average, about one atom of ordinary matter in a space the size of a closet — a cubic meter, give or take the fights about the second digit. A good vacuum pump on Earth is proud to do worse.

Insist on the second digit and the closet is a little emptier than the sentence suggests: about 0.2 ordinary atoms per cubic meter, once you weigh the universe properly and give the atoms only their share. “About one” is the kitchen insult. 0.2 is the receipt. Either way, you are not living in the average. You are living in a lump.

That is the catalog’s first insult. We live in a freak clump. The clump is not the rule. The rule is the emptiness.

The second insult is that the atoms are not even the main clump. When astronomers weigh the universe the way you weigh a truck — by how hard it pulls, not by how shiny it is — the atoms are a minority. About five percent of the energy budget is the stuff of chemistry. About a quarter is an extra pull that does not shine. About two-thirds is an extra shove that makes the stretch speed up. Chapters 17 through 19 are the inventory of those insults. Here it is enough to feel the loneliness of the five percent.

Elena weighs a bag of flour on a kitchen scale and trusts the number because the bag pushes down. She does not trust the number by how white the flour looks. Shine is a terrible scale. A bag of powdered sugar and a bag of whole wheat can fool the eye; they cannot fool the spring. Cosmology’s spring is gravity. Rotation speeds in the outskirts of galaxies, the way clusters lens background galaxies, the peaks in the leftover glow: those are pulls. The shine — stars, nebulae, the kitchen table — is the five percent announcing itself with light. Weigh the loaf by pull and the flour is a minority ingredient. Chapter 17 is the extra pull. Chapter 18 is the extra shove. Do not write those chapters here. Notice, only, that the review lead’s habit is the right one, and that the cosmos does not owe her a bag that matches the photograph.

The bookkeeping has a name. There is a critical density, *ρ_c*, the amount of stuff that would make the large-scale geometry sit on a knife-edge: neither curling closed toward a recollapse nor hinging open so fast that galaxies never find each other.

*ρ_c = 3H_0² / 8πG*

*where ρ_c is that critical density, H_0 is the stretch-rate of now, and G is Newton’s constant — the same G that weighs apples, planets, and the review lead’s flour.*

What we actually have is counted in units of that knife-edge:

*Ω = ρ / ρ_c*

*where Ω is a density parameter, a fraction of critical, and the pieces of Ω are allowed to add.*

The leftover glow, plus the stretch, plus the way structure grew, currently split the bill about like this: *Ω_b ≈ 0.05* in ordinary atoms (baryons), *Ω_c ≈ 0.27* in extra pull that does not shine, *Ω_Λ ≈ 0.68* in extra shove. The sum sits near 1, which is why “flat” keeps surviving the maps. Those decimals will move in the fourth digit. They will not swap the story that chemistry is a rounding error with a press agent.

Put a kitchen number on the knife-edge, because “critical density” sounds like a mood and it is a mass. For a Hubble constant near 70 kilometers per second per megaparsec, *ρ_c* is about 9 × 10⁻²⁷ kilograms per cubic meter — a few proton-masses in each empty closet if all of it were atoms, which it is not. Only the baryon slice, the 0.05, gets to be atoms, and that is how you arrive at 0.2 per cubic meter. The rest of the pull does not sit on the review lead’s scale as flour. The shove does not sit on it at all. “Flat” means the large-scale geometry is the one in which parallel lines stay parallel and the angles of a cosmic triangle add to 180 degrees. Planck’s maps like that answer. Part III will ask why the pebble sat on the blade. This chapter only weighs the bag.

Radiation is a rounding error *now*: the photon share, Ω_γ, is a few parts in ten thousand. Photons dominated the budget when the universe was young and the wavelengths were short; they lost the argument because each photon got tired as well as diluted. Neutrinos, decoupled at one second, are another small slice — a relic you feel in the helium and in the leftover glow’s peaks, not the extra pull of Chapter 17. The extra pull does not shine and does not, as far as the maps can tell, live in the periodic table. The extra shove does not dilute as matter does. Those two sentences are previews. They are not the chapters.

The 0.05 in Ω_b is not a guess from counting stars. Stars are a terrible census of atoms: most ordinary matter is not in stars. It is in hot gas, warm gas, a thin salt in clusters, a fog between galaxies. The leftover glow’s peaks and the three-minute deuterium thermostat agree on the baryon crowding to a level that would be vanity if they did not use different physics. That agreement is why 0.05 is hot enough to print. Counting kitchens will not get you there.

![Figure 11. A large cube. One black dot. A cubic meter of a smoothed cosmos.](Figures/figs/fig11.png)

Figure 11 is the most dishonest honest picture in the book. The cube is a meter on a side. The dot is an atom. If you drew the cube at the scale of the review lead’s kitchen, the next atom of the average cosmos would not be in the house. It would not be on the block. You would need a walk that feels like a mistake. We do not take that walk, because we live in the freak, and the freak is full of dots that stuck. The figure is what you get when you refuse the freak and insist on the climate.

Barrow liked to shock readers with billions, and the billions are still shocking if you let them be specific. The observable universe is about 93 billion light-years across, not because it is 93 billion years old — it is 13.8 — but because the stretch has been pulling the farthest light’s starting points farther while the light was in transit. Inside that sphere there are a few hundred billion galaxies you could, in a generous mood, name. Each galaxy has, give or take, a hundred billion stars. Multiply and you get a number that looks like a lie: on the order of 10²² stars. Then remember the closet with one atom. The stars are the rare weather. The emptiness is the climate.

The 93-billion-light-year sphere is a census volume, not a wall. Chapter 7 already told you why the number is larger than the age. This chapter only needs the inventory inside it. Ten-to-the-twenty-two is an order of magnitude, not a roll call: a hundred billion here, a hundred billion there, and the product is a 1 with twenty-two zeros and an argument about the first factor of ten. You may add a zero when the deep fields finish arguing about faint galaxies. You will not add emptiness to the stars and make the stars the rule. Figure 11 is the rule. The Milky Way is a rounding error that learned to shine.

You live in a freak clump twice. Once because a galaxy is already a conspiracy of gravity against the average. Again because a planet, a kitchen, a pair of lungs are conspiracies inside that conspiracy. Walk three meters from the review lead’s scale and you are still in the clump. Smooth the 93 billion light-years and you are back at 0.2 atoms per cubic meter and a dark that does not care about flour. The average cosmos has no tables. It has a leftover glow, a leftover helium, and a lot of nothing with a pull.

Compare the densities so the closet stays honest. Air in the review lead’s kitchen is about 2 × 10²⁵ molecules per cubic meter. The space between stars in the Galactic disk is already a vacuum by kitchen standards — maybe a million atoms per cubic meter in the better neighborhoods, far less in the halo. The smoothed cosmos is 0.2. Each step is a humiliation of the last. You cannot see 0.2 atoms per cubic meter. You can only weigh its consequences, or refuse to smooth, and live in the clump.

The clumps nest. A person is a clump in a kitchen. The kitchen is a clump on a planet. The planet is a clump around a star. The star is a clump in a galaxy. The galaxy is a clump in a group; our group is falling toward Virgo; Virgo sits in a larger web. Chapter 14 will grow the web from freckles. Here the point is only inventory: every noun you care about is a place where the average failed, gravity won, and the stretch was told to wait. The 10²² stars are 10²² local victories. They still occupy a negligible fraction of the 93-billion-light-year sphere.

If you could pour the observable universe’s ordinary atoms into the review lead’s style of bag, and you could find a scale that did not laugh, the bag would still be the five percent. The rest of the pull and all of the shove would not sit in the bag. That is why the catalog of exceptions — star, planet, kitchen, book — is not a catalog of the world. It is a catalog of the part of the world that can hold a reader.

Stars themselves are a later luxury. They are places where gravity won a local argument against the stretch, piled gas until the pile was hot enough to fuse, and then spent millions or billions of years turning hydrogen into the rest of the table. When the more massive ones die, they throw the table into space. Planets are the sediment of that throw. You are the sediment of the sediment, walking around asking where the sediment came from.

The catalog, then, is not a list of objects. It is a list of exceptions. A star is an exception to emptiness. A planet is an exception to the habit stars have of being unfriendly. A kitchen is an exception to the habit planets have of being dead. A book is an exception to the habit kitchens have of being used for casseroles.

the review lead’s bag of flour is an exception inside the exception. It has a weight she can read. The cosmos has a weight she cannot, except by the pull: the way a galaxy refuses to spin as the shine predicts, the way a cluster bends background light, the way the leftover glow’s peaks sit where a certain crowding of stuff requires. Weigh by shine and you will invent a universe made of kitchens. Weigh by pull and you will get the inventory above, and you will have to wait for Chapters 17 and 18 to name the majority ingredients. Waiting is allowed. Pretending the flour is the loaf is not.

The past we can rebuild explains how the exceptions got their raw material. It does not owe the exceptions a destiny. The leftover glow and the leftover helium will still be true if no one ever builds another kitchen. That is not a threat. It is the courtesy of a universe that does not need us in order to have had a first three minutes.

\newpage

# PART III — A Burst, Then Seeds

---

\newpage

## 12. Three Puzzles the Hot Bang Cannot Solve

The leftover glow and the leftover helium make the hot, expanding past look inevitable. They do not make it comfortable. Three puzzles sit in the model like bills on a table, and the three-minute workshop does not pay them.

The first is the *horizon problem*. Look at the microwave sky in two opposite directions. The temperatures match to a part in a hundred thousand. For two patches to agree that tightly, they usually need to have talked — to have exchanged light, mixed, come to terms. In the simple hot bang, those opposite patches have never been in each other’s past light cones. They are like two strangers who have never met and have never had a friend in common, wearing the same shirt to the same size. You can call it a coincidence. Physicists get hives.

The kitchen version is two thermometers.

Tape one to the wall above the stove. Tape the other to the wall by the back door. They agree to a part in 10⁵. In an ordinary kitchen that is not a miracle. Air mixed. Light bounced. The fan ran. The two walls have been in conversational range for hours. Now shrink the time the kitchen has existed until light from the stove has not yet had a chance to reach the door. The thermometers still agree. That is the horizon problem, moved off the sky and onto tile.

At 380,000 years, when the fog lifted and the leftover glow set out, a causal horizon — the farthest a light signal could have traveled since the simple bang began — was a small patch on the sky we photograph now. A couple of degrees. A thumbnail at arm’s length. The whole dome of the microwave sky is not a couple of degrees. Opposite patches sit a hundred thumbnails apart, and then some. The causal horizon at last scattering is much smaller than the sky. In the simple bang, the patches labeled A and B in Figure 12 have never been in each other’s past light cones. They did not mix. They did not come to terms. They still match, to one part in 10⁵, as if agreement were free.

You can try to rescue the simple bang by saying the universe started already even, on scales that had never talked. That is not a solution. That is a shrug wearing a lab coat. It pushes the evenness into the initial conditions and then looks innocent. Physics is supposed to *make* evenness, or at least to inherit it from a time when talking was possible. The simple bang, run backward from the leftover glow, does not give you that time. The leftover glow is a photograph of a single age, the entire sky at 380,000 years. The photograph is too even for the age.

Agreement, in physics, is not a fashion. Two patches of plasma come to the same temperature because photons ricochet between them until the hot spots have given their extra to the cold spots. That is thermal equilibrium, the same “we have mixed thoroughly” signature that makes the leftover glow a blackbody. Mixing needs a path. A path needs time. In the simple bang the time from a hot start to last scattering is 380,000 years, which sounds generous until you remember that the last-scattering surface we see is not 380,000 light-years across. Expansion has been stretching the rulers. The region that could have mixed by then subtends about a degree or two on today’s sky. Opposite patches — 180 degrees of argument — contain thousands of those little mixed rooms, none of which had a hallway to the others.

One part in 10⁵ is not a casual “pretty even.” It is the measured amplitude of the remaining freckles after the dipole of our own motion is subtracted. The average temperatures of opposite hemispheres match far tighter than a kitchen thermometer. Chapter 3’s light cones, applied at 380,000 years, do not overlap for A and B. Figure 12 is that non-overlap, drawn as a circle because the sky is a circle when you have only two dimensions of paper. The simple bang has no conversation that can buy the match. It has only a hope that the start was already a match. Hopes are not mechanisms.

The second is the *flatness problem*. The universe’s geometry — whether parallel lines stay parallel on the largest scales — is astonishingly close to “flat,” which is another way of saying the density is astonishingly close to a special critical value. In the simple bang, that closeness is unstable. A little too much density in the first second and the universe would have recollapsed before helium was done. A little too little and it would have become so empty, so fast, that galaxies would never have found each other. We live on a knife-edge that the equations keep trying to throw us off. Either someone balanced a pebble, or something *drove* the pebble to the middle of the blade.

Barrow liked a pebble on a blade. The blade is the critical density: the exact budget of stuff that makes *Ω = 1*, the universe in which the large-scale geometry does not curl into a ball or splay into a saddle. A little above, and gravity wins on a short clock. A little below, and the stretch wins so fast that clumps never find each other. We measure, today, a universe so close to that blade that the leftover glow, the later bombs of Chapter 18, and the frozen sound-scale in the galaxies all vote *flat* to within a percent or better. That is a fact about now. The insult is a fact about then.

In a decelerating bang — radiation, then matter, no burst — the quantity |Ω − 1| is driven *away* from zero as time runs forward. Closeness is unstable. Run the same equations toward the past and the required closeness blows up. By the time the clock reads one second, the pebble has to sit in the middle of the blade to something like one part in 10¹⁶. Not one part in a hundred. Not one part in a million. A 1-second pebble that would shame a watchmaker. Bump it, and you do not get a slightly different kitchen. You get a recollapse before helium, or a desert that never makes a galaxy.

Nobody balanced it with fingers. Either the initial conditions were an unpublished miracle, or something drove the pebble to the middle and held it there long enough that later physics would look inevitable. Inflation, in the next chapter, is the candidate drive. Here, notice only the bill: the simple bang does not explain the pebble. It assumes the pebble, and then spends 13.8 billion years trying to kick it off.

One second is not a mystic number. It is the door of Weinberg’s workshop: neutrinos have just decoupled, the neutron-to-proton ratio is freezing, the nuclear minutes are about to start. If the pebble is already wrong at that door, the workshop never opens, or opens onto a warehouse with no walls. The required closeness at the Planck time is worse — 10⁻⁶⁰, a number Barrow would have put on a chalkboard to watch a room go quiet. We do not need the Planck time to feel the insult. The 1-second pebble is rude enough. A pebble is not a mechanism either. It is a way of saying the tuning has a clock reading, and the clock reading is uncomfortably early.

The third is the *relic problem*. Grand unified theories — warm-to-cold ideas about how the forces were once one force — tend to produce leftover monsters: magnetic monopoles, heavy stable particles, knots in fields. The simple bang would have left the universe ankle-deep in them. We do not trip over monopoles on the way to the mailbox. Something diluted the relics, or they were never made in our past, or the grand theories are wrong. The first option is the one inflation was built to offer.

A magnetic monopole, in this context, is not a bar magnet you can put on the fridge. It is a knot in a grand-unified field, a particle that would be a north without a south, heavy, stable, and — if the simple bang passed through the grand-unified temperature — as common as the horizon would allow. One per causal patch, roughly, and then they sit there, too massive to decay, too stubborn to hide. They would outweigh the atoms. They would wreck the leftover helium’s accounting. They would be underfoot.

They are not underfoot. That is a fact you can take to the mailbox. So either the grand theories are wrong about the knots, or the knots were never cooked in our past, or something diluted them by a ridiculous factor — 10²⁵ in entropy, says the appendix, if you want a number to flinch at. Inflation was built, in part, as a dilution machine. Guth was thinking about monopoles when he started. The even sky and the pebble came along as bonuses. The bonuses are now the famous posters. The monopoles are the quiet third bill.

![Figure 12. A circle for the last-scattering sky. Two opposite patches, A and B. A short mark showing they never met in the simple bang.](Figures/figs/fig12.png)

Imagine the kitchen table at the end of Weinberg’s workshop.

Three envelopes were already paid. The leftover glow exists. The leftover helium exists. The stretch exists. Those are receipts. You can put them in a drawer. Then three more envelopes sit where the casserole should be, unpaid, the kind of bills that do not go away because you cooked a good dinner. One says HORIZON. One says FLATNESS. One says RELICS. The three-minute workshop did not write checks for them. It was not that kind of workshop. It reconstructed a soup. It did not explain why the soup’s bowl was so round, so even, and so empty of monsters.

Weinberg paid the nuclear minutes. He paid them with the same nuclear physics we still use in stars and in laboratories, and the helium still sits in the account. These three remain. They are not evidence that the workshop was a fiction. They are evidence that the workshop started with a table already set, and nobody at the table could say who set it.

None of these puzzles says the hot bang is false. They say it is incomplete as a story of *initial conditions*. Weinberg could reconstruct the nuclear minutes without knowing why the sky was so even. Guth, in 1980, asked whether a brief, violent stretch *before* those minutes could set the table so the minutes would look inevitable.

Guth was not, in 1980, handing the community a finished preface. He was a particle theorist staring at monopoles, asking whether a false-vacuum stretch could dilute them, and noticing that the same stretch would also even the sky and drive the pebble. The paper is a question with a mechanism attached, not a verdict. Old inflation, the first mechanism, will fail to exit. That failure is Chapter 13’s opening debt. The question survives the debt. The three bills are still on the table either way.

That question is the opening of Part III. It is not yet the solution. Guth’s first try will have a graceful-exit problem, a mess about how to stop, which Chapter 13 will have to clean. The sky will later be photographed in polarization by people who were sometimes too eager. Those fights belong to the next pages. Here the honest inventory is three unpaid bills, a paid nuclear dinner, and a physicist asking whether a burst could be the missing payment.

That is a different kind of move. The three minutes are a reconstruction. Inflation is a proposed preface. Prefaces can be wrong even when the book is right. Keep the temperatures straight. The puzzles are hot. The leading solution is warm. The details of the preface — what field, what potential, what first 10⁻³⁶ second — are still being written in pencil.

---

\newpage

## 13. Inflation, Without the Hype

Here is inflation without the poster.

Suppose, in a sliver of time so short that “sliver” is already too romantic, a patch of the universe was filled with a field that behaved like a temporary cosmological constant: a stuff whose tension was so odd that gravity, locally, *repelled*. The stretch that matters is an e-fold: every distance grows by a factor of *e*, about 2.7, not by a tidy doubling. About fifty to sixty of those e-folds are what the job asks for. Distances that had been smaller than a proton became larger than a grapefruit, then larger than a solar system, then larger than the part of the universe we can see. Then the field decayed. Its energy dumped into particles. The dump is called reheating, which is a calm word for setting a universe on fire. After the dump, you have a hot bang: soup, light, the three minutes, the leftover glow.

The kitchen is a pot on a high flame.

For a sliver the pot does not simmer. It *boils over*: the volume of steam is not a polite trickle, it is a room-filling insult, doubling and doubling. Then the whole violent pot dumps into a larger pot of soup. The dump is reheating. After the dump you have the hot bang. The boil-over is not the soup. The soup is what you can reconstruct. The boil-over is the proposed preface that makes the soup’s evenness less of a miracle.

The count that matters is *e-folds*. Each e-fold stretches every distance by a factor of *e*, about 2.7. A doubling would be a different count, and this chapter will not teach the wrong one. About 50 to 60 e-folds are what the three puzzles ask for: enough stretch that the whole sky we photograph was once inside one talking neighborhood, enough that a curved surface looks flat, enough that one relic per pre-burst patch becomes one relic per observable universe, or fewer. Sixty of them take a proton-scale patch and throw it past everything we can see. The clock for the whole insult is of order 10⁻³² seconds. That number is a round order, not a kitchen timer. It depends on how hard the field was shoving. It is short the way a lightning strike is short, except lightning is leisurely.

Slow-roll is how the pot is allowed to boil that long without instantly dumping. If the field sprinted down its hill, the boil-over would be over before it had stretched enough, or it would dump in a mess of bubbles. Slow-roll is a hill so flat the field creeps. Hubble friction acts like a damper. Sixty e-folds of almost-constant shove, then a dump. The field that does this job is called the inflaton because physics names things by their chores. We have not met it in a detector. It is unnamed in the only sense that matters: no mass, no charge, no track in a tank.

Reheating deserves a slower look, because the dump is where the preface becomes the book we already trust.

While the field is slow-rolling, its energy is locked in the odd tension: vacuum-like, smooth, shoving. When the field reaches the bottom of the hill it oscillates. Oscillation is particles, or at least the right to become particles. Those particles collide, thermalize, and you have a radiation bath — the hot bang, at last, with a temperature that depends on how the dump was done. The reheating temperature is model-dependent. The appendix will not apologize for that. What is not model-dependent is the order of operations: burst, dump, soup, three minutes, leftover glow. You do not get to skip the dump and still claim the helium. The helium is soup-work. The burst is table-setting.

Fifty to sixty e-folds is a window, not a sacred count. Too few, and a corner of today’s sky was never inside the talking neighborhood; the horizon bill stays unpaid. Too many is allowed by the local job description and starts to grow the rumor of Chapter 15: extra stretch somewhere else, extra rooms, a pot that does not stop. The local sky asks for about sixty. It does not ask for infinity. Do not let infinity borrow sixty’s paycheck.

Picture two kitchens, two years apart. In the first, 1980, a pot is boiling so violently that bubbles of “done” soup nucleate in a superheated sea and never quite fill the pot; the walls between bubbles are a mess. That is old inflation, Guth’s first try, the graceful-exit problem as a cooking failure. In the second, 1982, the flame is high but the pot is a wide, almost-flat pan. The whole surface comes up to temperature together and then, when the field finds the lip, dumps as one. That is slow-roll. Linde’s pan and Albrecht–Steinhardt’s pan were independent. Physics sometimes invents the same dish twice in one year when the bill is loud enough.

Why bother? Because the three puzzles go pale.

The opposite patches of sky *did* talk. They talked when they were neighbors, before the burst pulled them out of conversational range. The burst then stretched their agreement across the whole sky. The horizon problem becomes a memory of an old neighborhood.

The knife-edge of flatness gets driven toward the middle. Stretch a curved surface enough and any small patch looks flat. A basketball, to an ant the size of an atom, is a plane. Inflation is the move that makes us the ant.

The relics get diluted. One monopole per pre-burst patch becomes one monopole per observable universe, or fewer. We stop tripping.

That is the job description. It is not yet a proof. A preface that pays three bills is a good preface. It can still be the wrong preface, or the right job done by a field we have not named.

Repulsive, here, is not a personality. Einstein’s gravity, for ordinary stuff, pulls. For a stuff whose pressure is negative enough — a cosmological-constant-like tension — the same equations shove. The burst is that shove, brief and huge. It is not anti-gravity in a comic-book sense. It is the Friedmann equation with a budget that has the wrong sign for anyone who grew up on apples. Then the budget changes, because the field rolls and dumps, and apples make sense again. Figure 13 is that change of budget: a steep rise labeled burst, then a gentler slope labeled after. The after is the hot bang we already rebuilt. The rise is the proposed reason the after looks so unsurprising.

![Figure 13. One curve: a steep rise labeled burst, then a gentler slope labeled after.](Figures/figs/fig13.png)

Alan Guth’s first version had a graceful-exit problem: the burst did not know how to stop without making a mess. Andrei Linde, and independently Albrecht and Steinhardt, found a smoother hill for the field to roll down — *slow-roll*. Linde later suggested that the starting values of the field could be chaotic, different in different places, some patches inflating a lot, some not. That idea will grow a maybe-multiverse in Chapter 15. Do not let it jump the line. The *local* burst is the working theory. The froth of other bursts is a rumor the local burst can tell if you let it drink.

The graceful-exit problem, said without kindness, is this. Guth’s 1980 boil-over happened in pockets. True-vacuum bubbles nucleated in a false-vacuum sea and did not fill space without leaving walls and wreckage. The burst that was supposed to set the table set a junkyard instead. In 1982, Linde, and independently Albrecht and Steinhardt, flattened the hill. The whole patch rolls slowly together. New inflation, slow-roll. That is the working picture, the one whose job description still fits the leftover glow. Old inflation is a historical kindness we owe Guth for asking the question. It is not the pot we cook with.

What inflation has not done: name the field. We have no particle in a detector called the inflaton. We have a shape that works and a sky that looks like the shape predicted. That is more than a mood and less than a conviction. Appendix A13 writes the e-folds and the slow-roll parameters. Here it is enough to know that “inflation” is not a brand. It is a job description: a brief, repulsive stretch that sets the table for the hot bang we can actually reconstruct.

A humility from 2014 belongs on this page, because poster-inflation hates humility and the sky does not.

A telescope at the South Pole, BICEP2, looked for swirls in the leftover glow — B-modes — that a burst would have printed with gravitational waves. In 2014 the team announced a detection. The world did a lap. Warm claim, briefly worn as hot. Then dust in our own galaxy, polarized dust, sat down in the same seat as the signal. A 2015 joint analysis with Planck was the kill. The swirl was not the burst. The swirl was grit in the kitchen, the kind that lives in the Milky Way and polarizes starlight for a living. Humility is not a personality trait in a press office. It is what the sky did when someone claimed to have photographed the first 10⁻³² second.

The kill is worth teaching because it is how a warm claim dies correctly.

B-modes are a swirl pattern in the polarization of the leftover glow. Inflationary gravitational waves would write one kind of swirl, a primordial kind, whose amplitude is a number called *r*, the tensor-to-scalar ratio. Dust writes another kind, because dust grains in our galaxy align with magnetic fields and scatter starlight with a handedness of their own. BICEP2 looked at a clean-looking patch of southern sky at 150 GHz and saw swirls. Planck, looking at many frequencies, could tell dust from glow by color. Dust is redder in a specific way. The joint analysis showed the color of dust. *r* was not measured. An upper limit remained. Upper limits are not nothing. They are a fence the hill has to live behind. They are also not a detection. The poster that said first direct evidence had to be taken down. Taking it down is part of the science. Leaving it up would have been a different book.

What survived the dust is quieter and better. Planck measured the tilt of the primordial spectrum: *n_s* ≈ 0.965, a little redder than a perfectly scale-invariant 1. Slow-roll likes that song. A hill that is not perfectly flat, a field that creeps, a spectrum that leans a little toward larger scales: that is the shape, not the photograph of a particle. A tilt is a clue. Clues are allowed to be warm. They are not allowed to be a victory lap.

String theory sometimes offers inflatons the way a catalog offers furniture. The catalog is large. **It cannot currently be tested.** We will not pay the book’s rent with a catalog.

A catalog is tempting because the job description is wide. Many hills slow-roll. Many fields, in many theories, can pretend to be a temporary cosmological constant for 10⁻³² seconds and then dump. String theory’s catalog is famous for being large. Large is not the same as found. A furniture catalog does not furnish a kitchen. Until a particular hill is killed or confirmed by a number — a value of *r*, a feature in *P(k)* the leftover glow does not have, a leftover particle in a tank — we have a job, not a hire. The unnamed inflaton is not a failure of imagination. It is a failure of detection. Failures of detection are allowed to sit on the table beside the soup. They are not allowed to be called dinner.

Keep the temperatures straight on this chapter too. Hot: the three puzzles are real, and a brief stretch of the sort described would pale them. Warm: slow-roll inflation, 50 to 60 e-folds, a dump into soup, a tilt near 0.965, is the working preface. Cold: we have named the field, bagged it in a tank, and photographed its gravitational waves. We have not. The pot boiled over. We are eating the soup. The name of the cook is still a blank line.

---

\newpage

## 14. How a Quantum Twitch Became a Galaxy

If inflation had stretched the universe perfectly smooth, there would be nothing to look at. No galaxies, no stars, no kitchens. Perfection is sterile. The universe is saved by a twitch.

Quantum fields cannot sit still. Even in their quietest state they jitter. During the burst, those jitters — differences in the field from place to place, at the smallest scale the quantum rules allow — get stretched from subatomic to cosmic the way a note written on a balloon becomes unreadable when you inflate it, except here the note becomes a *continent*. A region that jittered a little dense stays a little dense. After reheating, gravity, which has all the time in the world, starts calling in the debt. The dense patches pull. The thin patches get thinner. After a few hundred million years, the dense patches are making the first stars. After a few billion, they are making galaxies that look, cropped and gray, like Figure 14.

The kitchen mistake is to think of a twitch as a metaphor for “something small.” It is not a metaphor. An electron in a wire does not have a definite path. The same family of rule that forbids that path forbids a quantum field from sitting at one exact value in every place. Inflation is not a new kind of uncertainty. It is an amplifier. It takes the jitter the quantum rules already insist on and stretches it until the jitter is the size of a future cluster. Gravity is the sculptor. Gravity does not invent the pattern. Gravity spends 13.8 billion years pulling on a pattern that was already written, faintly, in a field that could not sit still.

This is Mukhanov’s territory, and Guth’s, and a workshop of people in 1982 who realized that inflation does not just solve puzzles. It *makes the seeds*. The leftover glow’s freckles are those seeds, photographed at 380,000 years, before gravity had done much sculpting. The pattern has a sound: almost the same power at every scale, a little redder on large scales, a spectrum written *P(k)* in the appendix and “almost scale-invariant” in conversation. Planck measured the tilt. The tilt matches slow-roll inflation’s favorite song. That is the warm claim at its warmest.

The 1982 workshop — a room of chalk, not a cathedral — is worth a pause because this is the moment the burst stopped being only a bill-payer. Viatcheslav Mukhanov, with G. V. Chibisov a year earlier, and then a crowd that included Hawking, Starobinsky, Guth, Turner, Bardeen, Steinhardt, had the same rude thought: if a field is jittering while it stretches, the jitter *is* a spectrum of density. After the dump into soup, that spectrum is a set of slightly overdense and underdense patches. The leftover glow is the photograph of those patches as temperature. Galaxies are the same patches, later, as matter. One twitch. Two exhibits.

The numbers are small on purpose. The amplitude of the primordial spectrum, *A_s*, is about 2 × 10⁻⁹. Two parts in a billion. That is how loud the twitch was, as a fractional difference in density, when it was stretched beyond the causal horizon and frozen. The tilt is *n_s* ≈ 0.965, Planck’s digit, a hair redder than 1. If *n_s* were exactly 1, every scale would have been given the same fractional power. Slow-roll does not quite do that. The hill is not perfectly flat, so large scales, which exit the horizon earlier, get a little more to work with. The leftover glow’s freckles sing that song. The song is not a galaxy. It is the score gravity will play.

*P(k)* is a recipe for how much power lives at each spatial scale. Scale-invariant means the fractional jitter looks the same if you zoom in or out, like a coastline that is wrinkly at every magnification. Almost scale-invariant means the coastline is a little calmer when you zoom into small bays, a little wilder when you back up to the whole shore. That is the red tilt. *A_s* ~ 2 × 10⁻⁹ sets how wrinkly, overall, the coastline was. Gravity turns wrinkle into continent. The leftover glow is the coastline at 380,000 years, still a coastline, not yet a continent. Figure 14 is a continent.

During the burst, a jitter of a given size is stretched until it is larger than the causal horizon — it *exits*. Outside the horizon it freezes: the patch cannot talk to its neighbors, so the overdensity stays an overdensity. After the burst, the horizon grows again, and the jitter *re-enters*. Gravity can then start sculpting. Different scales exit at different times during the 50 to 60 e-folds, which is why the spectrum is a story about a hill, not a single stamp. Large scales exit earlier. If the hill is slowly changing, large scales get a slightly different amplitude. That slight difference is *n_s* − 1. Planck’s 0.965 is a sentence about the hill’s slope, written in the leftover glow.

Mukhanov’s name is on the variable that lets you quantize the twitch in an expanding background — a combination of field jitter and metric jitter that the appendix will write as if it were a friend. You do not need the friend to understand the claim. You need this: the same quantum rule that smears an electron’s path smears the unnamed field’s value. The burst is a loudspeaker. Gravity is a chisel. The 1982 workshop is the moment a room of people heard the loudspeaker and realized they were also looking at a sculpture garden.

![Figure 14. One nearby spiral, disk filling the frame. A quantum twitch, stretched and pulled for thirteen billion years.](Figures/figs/fig14.jpg)

A detail that should not be allowed to stay abstract: the twitch is not a metaphor for “something small.” It is the same kind of uncertainty that keeps an electron from having a definite path in a wire. Inflation is the amplifier. Gravity is the sculptor. You are a later exhibit.

The timeline of exhibits, kept at the right temperature:

Hot: the leftover glow has freckles whose statistics we have measured. Their amplitude and tilt are numbers, not adjectives.

Warm: those freckles are the stretched jitter, and gravity grew them. The first stars, in this working picture, light up around 100 to 200 million years after the dump — a range, not a birthday. JWST photographs later exhibits: galaxies already assembled, already chemically rude, already sitting on the web that Chapter 16 will name. Those photographs are not a laboratory of the twitch. They are the museum downstairs from the laboratory.

The first stars are a warm chapter in a hot book. Theory says the first lumps that can cool and collapse do so around 100 to 200 million years after the dump, in minihalos that would look, if you could see them, like failed galaxies: a few hundred thousand or a few million solar masses of extra pull, a handful of massive, short-lived, metal-free stars. We have not photographed those stars as individuals. We have photographed, with JWST, galaxies that are already later exhibits — assembled, chemically polluted, sitting at redshifts that still make the stretch look like a time machine. Those galaxies are not the twitch. They are the twitch after gravity has had a few hundred million years to work and after the first stars have already lived and died and thrown carbon into the kitchen. Do not let a sharp infrared image pose as a laboratory of quantum jitter. It is a museum label that says LATER.

Gravity as sculptor is not a metaphor you can retire. Overdense patches pull. Underdense patches get emptied. On small scales the extra pull of Chapter 17 helps the lumps hold together against the stretch. On large scales the stretch eventually wins, and the web freezes. In between, for a few billion years, the sculptor is busy. A spiral disk is a late, local luxury: gas that cooled, spun, and settled in a halo that the twitch had already chosen. The choice was a nick of size 10⁻⁵ in the leftover glow. The luxury took the rest of cosmic time.

Cold: we have watched a twitch become a spiral in a laboratory. We have not. We have watched the intermediate photograph — the leftover glow at 380,000 years — and done the math that grows a 2 × 10⁻⁹ jitter into a disk like Figure 14. That is allowed to be enough, if we say so.

Chaos belongs here as mechanism. A jitter of one part in a hundred thousand is nothing in a kitchen and everything in a cosmos.
