Change the jitter’s pattern and you get a different web of galaxies — still a web, different rooms. That is not destiny. It is sensitivity. Chapter 16 will give chaos its own chair. Here, notice that the most important structures you will ever see began as noise.

Do not let the spiral in Figure 14 pose as a time-lapse of a quantum event. It is a nearby galaxy, light a few tens of millions of years old, a late sculpture. The twitch that seeded the line of lumps it grew from is not in the frame. The twitch is in the statistics of the leftover glow, and in the fact that a universe this empty still managed to make a disk this pretty. Gravity had time. Time is the sculptor’s only luxury. The quantum rule supplied the chisel’s first nick.

Between the freckles and the first stars there is a long, dark kitchen.

Last scattering is 380,000 years. The first stars, warm, are 100 to 200 million years. In between, the universe is not empty of stuff. It is empty of light worth a name. Neutral hydrogen, leftover helium, the extra pull gathering in the dark, the stretch still going. Lumps that were 10⁻⁵ too dense in the glow become, slowly, lumps that can cool. Cooling is the unglamorous step. Gravity pulls. Gas that can radiate away its heat can keep falling. Gas that cannot stays puffy and fails. The first stars are the first successful coolers, not the first lumps. JWST’s later exhibits have already inherited metals from coolers that died. That inheritance is why a sharp galaxy at high redshift is not a photograph of the nick. It is a photograph of a kitchen after several meals.

You cannot, in a laboratory, stretch a quantum jitter until it is a spiral and then wait out the sculptor. The amplifier is a burst we cannot turn on. The sculptor’s hours are cosmological. What we can do is measure the intermediate photograph, write *P(k)* with *A_s* ~ 2 × 10⁻⁹ and *n_s* ≈ 0.965, and check that gravity, given that score, grows a web that looks like the surveys of Chapter 16 and a disk that looks like Figure 14. That check is the warm claim at its most responsible. It is not a time-lapse. It is a courtroom with two exhibits and a lot of math between them.

Hot claim: the leftover glow has a pattern of freckles whose statistics we have measured. Warm claim: those freckles are stretched quantum jitters, grown by gravity into galaxies. Cold claim: we have watched a twitch become a spiral in a laboratory. We have not. We have watched the intermediate photograph and done the math. That is allowed to be enough, if we say so.

---

\newpage

## 15. Bubbles That May Never Stop

Once you have a field that can burst, you can ask whether the bursting ever takes a holiday.

In many versions of slow-roll, the field reaches the bottom of its hill in some places and keeps rolling, or keeps jittering, in others. The places that keep going keep stretching. The places that stop become hot bangs — pocket universes, bubbles, whatever noun you can stand. Our observable sky would be inside one stopped place. Outside, the bursting might still be hiring.

This is eternal inflation, Linde’s and Vilenkin’s child, Guth’s grown rumor. It is a live hypothesis. It is not a photograph.

The kitchen is a pot that keeps boiling in the next room.

You live in the room where the pot already dumped into soup. The soup is the leftover glow, the leftover helium, the stretch, the galaxies. In the next room — a room whose door is not a door, because the stretch between rooms is not a hallway you can walk — the original pot may still be boiling over. New dumps, new soups, new leftover glows that are not ours. You cannot go there. A bubble is not a destination. It is not a planet with a pad. It is a volume that reheated, or a volume that has not, separated from you by a stretch that does not sell tickets. Chapter 25 will talk about greenhouses on worlds you can, in principle, point at. This chapter is not that chapter. Bubbles are a maybe about the loaf, not a travel brochure.

A destination has a worldline you can aim at. A bubble that nucleated elsewhere, behind a stretch that has been doubling for longer than our soup has existed, does not. Light from that bubble does not arrive. A ship does not arrive. The word “neighbor” in the cold claim at the end of this chapter is a courtesy and a trap. Neighbors, on Earth, share a street. These would share only a mathematics.

In many slow-roll models the quantum jitter in the field, per Hubble time, can be larger than the classical roll down the hill. Where the jitter kicks the field *up* the hill, that patch stretches more and the hill remains. Where it kicks down, that patch dumps into soup and becomes a bubble. The stretching patches make more volume than the dumping patches. Volume wins. The boil-over in the next room does not stop because the next room keeps being remade. That is eternal inflation as a mechanism, not as a mood. Linde’s picture is often this self-reproduction on a hill. Vilenkin’s picture is often bubbles nucleating in a false-vacuum sea. The nouns differ. The next-room pot is the same insult: the bursting may not take a holiday.

If the underlying physics has many ways to stop — many valleys for the field to fall into, many sets of effective constants — then different bubbles can freeze out with different laws. That is the expensive copy machine of Chapter 39, and it is where the anthropic filter of Chapter 41 gets its only real job. Weinberg’s argument that the cosmological constant should not be much larger than what still allows galaxies *needs* an ensemble. Eternal inflation plus a landscape is the ensemble people reach for.

Do not write the filter here. Chapter 41 will have to say, carefully, what an ensemble is allowed to explain and what it is only allowed to excuse. Here it is enough to see why Weinberg’s Λ is lonely without company. A vacuum energy — a hum of energy that empty space carries even with nothing in it — as small as the one we weigh is a joke if it is a unique, first-principles output of a theory. It is less of a joke if many vacua are tried and we can only find ourselves in one that still makes galaxies. That sentence is a pointer, not a proof. The pointer needs two things this chapter can name and cannot certify: a bursting that never quite stops, and a menu of valleys. The bursting is eternal inflation, Linde and Vilenkin, a live maybe. The menu, in its most advertised form, is a landscape inherited from string theory.

Weinberg, in 1987, asked how large a cosmological constant could be before galaxies fail to form. The answer is: not much larger than what we weigh. That is an upper bound from the existence of galaxies, not a calculation of the vacuum from first principles. An upper bound of that kind is idle unless many values are tried. One universe, one Λ, and Weinberg’s bound is a coincidence we live inside. Many bubbles, many Λs, and the bound becomes a filter: we could not have found ourselves in the bubbles that shoved too hard, too soon. Chapter 41 is the filter chapter. This chapter only points at the drawer the filter would need. Do not open the drawer and call it a house. Do not write the filter’s sentences here. Point, and leave the pointing visible.

The landscape, in its most advertised form, comes from string theory. **String theory cannot currently be tested.** A landscape of valleys inherited from an untestable framework is not a measurement. It is a maybe stacked on a maybe. This book will let the stack sit on the table. It will not serve it for dinner.

![Figure 15. Three large circles in a spreading field. A picture of a possibility.](Figures/figs/fig15.png)

Figure 15 is a picture of a possibility. It is not a map. Maps have distances you can travel. These circles do not.

If you find yourself packing a bag for a bubble, put the bag down. Chapter 6 already took away a single now. Chapter 3 already made light late on purpose. A bubble that is not in your past light cone is not late mail. It is elsewhere, in the technical sense, and then some: the stretch between dumped soups can put them out of every future cone you will ever occupy. That is why bubbles are not destinations. Destinations, even cruel ones, sit on a worldline you can still draw. These do not.

There is a further rumor: that the bursting has no beginning, that bubble has always begotten bubble. The Borde–Guth–Vilenkin result says otherwise for a wide class of stretching spacetimes: if the average expansion is positive, the spacetime is past-incomplete. Eternal inflation, even if it has no end, still faces a boundary in the past. The bubbles do not give you a free forever-behind-you. They give you a messier loaf.

Past-incomplete, in the kitchen, means this. Follow a worldline backward through a spacetime that has been stretching, on average, and you run out of geodesic — the straightest path the geometry allows, the trail a free stone or a free clock actually follows — before you run out of curiosity. You do not get a quiet eternity of boiling behind you. You get a boundary, a place where the description stops and a different description — quantum gravity, a bounce, a sentence we do not own — would have to take over. BGV is not a proof that the loaf had a kitchen-clock beginning. It is a proof that *this* kind of forever-behind-you is not for free. The next-room pot does not pay the first-word bill. Chapter 44 will sit with that bill. Here: eternal inflation is not a machine for manufacturing a past without an edge.

Measure problem, in one sentence: if there are infinitely many bubbles and infinitely many observers, the word “typical” breaks. You cannot say “most universes are like this” until you say how you count. No one has a count that everyone else has to accept. Until they do, “the multiverse predicts X” is a hope in a lab coat.

Volume-weighted counting rewards the patches that stretched longest. Observer-weighted counting tries to ask where a typical observer sits. Pocket-weighted counting treats each dumped soup as one. They disagree. They disagree about whether we are typical, whether the cosmological constant we weigh is typical, whether anything is typical. An ensemble without a measure is a drawer of photographs with no rule for which one is “the” photograph. Chapter 41 will need a rule if it wants the filter to be more than a mood. This chapter’s job is to admit that the drawer is a rumor, and that the rumor is not a destination.

Infinity is the rude guest. If the next-room pot never stops, there are infinitely many dumped soups. Infinitely many observers. Infinitely many values of anything you care to count. A ratio of infinities is not a prediction until you say which infinity sits in the denominator. Different measures are different denominators. They are not a minor bookkeeping quarrel. They change what the rumor is allowed to claim. Until one of them is forced on us by a principle we already trust, “the multiverse predicts X” should be read as “some ways of counting would like X.” That is a thinner sentence. It is the honest one.

Past-incompleteness and the measure problem are not the same bill. BGV is about the loaf having an edge behind you. The measure is about not knowing how to average the rooms in front of you, or beside you, if the boiling never stops. You can have an edge in the past and an uncountable froth in the future. That combination is allowed. It is not a comfort. It is a messier loaf, as the earlier paragraph said, and messier is not the same as mapped.

Warm claim: inflation *can* be eternal in many models. Cold claim: it is, and we live in a bubble with neighbors we will never meet. The sky we have does not require the neighbors. It permits them. Permission is not a postcard.

Keep the next-room pot on a back burner, not on the dining table. The local burst of Chapter 13 is the working preface to the soup we can taste. The pot that may still be boiling elsewhere is a maybe that the local burst can tell if you let it drink. Letting it drink is allowed. Paying rent with the drink is not.

The next-room pot is also a humility about nouns.

People hear “bubble” and picture a place, a film of soap, a destination with an inside you could visit if you had a better ship. The picture is a liar in three ways. First, the wall of a nucleated bubble, in the versions that have walls, is not a surface you approach. It is a stretch you cannot catch. Second, other dumped soups need not share our leftover glow, our helium fraction, our shove. Different valleys, different effective constants: that is the landscape rumor, and the rumor is untestable while it is borrowed from string theory. Third, even a bubble that dumped with laws like ours is not “next door.” Next door is a kitchen word. These rooms do not share a hall.

Guth’s grown rumor, Linde’s child, Vilenkin’s child: the bursting as a process that, once started, makes more of itself. That is the live hypothesis. It may be false. Slow-roll can be arranged, in some hills, so that the classical roll always beats the jitter and the pot dumps everywhere at once. Those hills pay Chapter 13’s bills without hiring Chapter 15. The sky we have does not yet force the hiring. It permits it. Permission, again, is not a postcard. Figure 15 remains a picture of a possibility. Frame it as a possibility and the chapter has done its job. Frame it as a map of destinations and the chapter has failed.

---

\newpage

## 16. Chaos Is an Engine

Chaos, in physics, is not a mood and not a mess. It is a precise kind of sensitivity: two starts that differ by a breath end in rooms that do not recognize each other. Weather is chaotic. A pendulum swinging over a magnet can be chaotic. The approach to a final crunch, if the universe had been headed for one, would have been chaotic — Barrow proved things in that vicinity, and Belinski, Khalatnikov, and Lifshitz gave the mixmaster a name. A recollapsing cosmos would not politely reverse the film. It would scramble.

The kitchen is a dropped tray of yeast.

Same flour. Same water. Same yeast. The tray hits the floor and the grains go everywhere. Two bakers, starting from two almost identical spills, do not bake the same loaf. One kitchen gets a dense crumb. The other gets holes like a sponge. The ingredients did not change. The sensitivity did the work. Lyapunov, if you want the surname on the sensitivity, is the person who taught mathematics to measure how fast nearby starts stop being nearby. A Lyapunov time is a clock for forgetting. Weather forgets in days. A mixmaster crunch would forget in a scramble of axes. A quantum twitch, stretched and then pulled by gravity for 13.8 billion years, forgets the subatomic room and remembers only a web.

Take two trays. Spill them as identically as a human can. Watch. In a Lyapunov system the difference that was a grain of yeast becomes, after enough doublings of error, a different city of holes in the crumb. The number that measures the doubling is a Lyapunov exponent; its inverse is a time. Weather’s time is short, which is why the kitchen radio stops pretending on day ten. The stretch’s time, for the average expansion, is long, which is why Friedmann still works as a forecast. The mixmaster’s time, near a crunch, is short in the only way that matters: too short to steer. Nearby starts, unrecognizable rooms: that is the whole definition, with a surname attached so nobody can pretend chaos meant “messy feelings.”

At the beginning, chaos is an engine.

The quantum twitch of Chapter 14 is sensitivity as a gift. Stretch it, and you get a web. The cosmic web — filaments of galaxies around voids like holes in a sponge — is what gravity does with a nearly scale-invariant jitter after thirteen billion years. It is not a design. It is what you get when pull is allowed to run on noise.

Same engine, two temperatures. At the start, sensitivity is how a nick of size 10⁻⁵ becomes a sponge you can survey. At a hypothetical crunch, sensitivity is how a last decimal becomes a mixer nobody holds. The mathematics is cousin to itself. The moral is not. One gift. One theft. Do not let a poster collapse them into “chaos means we cannot know anything.” We can know the leftover glow. We can know the web’s family. We cannot know, from a grain of yeast, which hole in which loaf. That is the precise kind of not-knowing. It is enough.

The web is not a drawing. It is a survey. The Sloan Digital Sky Survey and the Two-degree-Field survey mapped enough galaxies that the sponge became a fact you could crop and print. Figure 16 is that crop: filaments as dark on light, voids as the light, a later exhibit of the same jitter that freckled the leftover glow. Two nearby starts in the primordial noise — two almost identical spills of yeast — would not grow the same rooms. They would still grow a web. Still a sponge. Different holes. That is chaos as an engine: the family of outcomes is stable, the particular kitchen is not.

A survey is a census, not an anecdote. 2dF, from Australia, and SDSS, from a telescope in New Mexico, redshifted enough galaxies that the sponge stopped being a theorist’s sketch. Filaments, walls, voids: the same family of pattern the leftover glow’s freckles asked for, grown. You can still argue about the second digit of how much extra pull sits in the filaments. You cannot argue that the web is a mood. Figure 16 is a measurement, cropped so a page can hold it. The yeast on the floor is the same measurement, earlier, before gravity baked. Same ingredients. Different loaves, if you spilled twice. One loaf, because we spilled once.

![Figure 16. A cropped survey map of galaxies, thresholded so the filaments read as dark on light. A web grown from twitches.](Figures/figs/fig16.jpg)

Barrow’s habit, useful here, was to walk you up to a large number and then refuse to call the number a destiny. The web is a large fact. It is not a plan. Mixmaster is a large fact about a kind of ending we do not appear to be headed for. It is not a control panel.

The mixmaster, named for a kitchen mixer because the axes of a collapsing universe would stretch and squash in a chaotic rotation, is Belinski, Khalatnikov, and Lifshitz: BKL. Near a spacelike singularity — a place, Chapter 24's proper subject, where the loaf tears and the equations stop describing what happens next — the generic behavior in general relativity is not a polite, spherical crunch. It is an oscillation of shapes, sensitive to the last decimal, a Lyapunov nightmare. Barrow proved things in that vicinity that matter for anyone who wants a last moment to be a steering wheel. Nearby starts become unrecognizable rooms. A civilization of perfect computers would not find a handle. The geometry would melt the handle.

Picture a collapsing room whose walls do not approach you together. One axis crunches while another stretches, then they trade, then they trade again, the sequence sensitive to the last decimal of the starting shape. That is Mixmaster, Bianchi IX in the jargon, a kitchen mixer beating a dough that is the geometry itself. BKL argued this is generic: not a special, spherical collapse, but the thing Einstein’s theory wants to do when a spacelike singularity is approached. Barrow took that generic chaos seriously enough to ask what a final intelligent observer could still control. The answer, in the vicinity of those proofs, is: almost nothing, unless you add extra structure the known laws do not hand you. Ideas for ideas can add the structure. Data have not.

At the end, if there were a crunch, the same family of sensitivity would be a theft. Tiny differences near a final singularity would be amplified until no one, not even a civilization of perfect computers, could steer. That fact will matter when someone tries to sell you a required Omega Point: a last moment in which mind takes the controls and computes forever. Chaos at the crunch is not a control panel. It is a warning that control panels melt.

Chapter 19 will finish that sale’s refusal on other grounds: the shove is winning, the bombs of 1998 are not a crunch track. Even in a universe that *did* crunch, BKL plus Barrow would still be waiting at the far end with a scrambled mixer. Forever-mind fails twice. Once because the data do not crunch. Once because a crunch, if you had one, would not be a computer. Do not let the Omega Point borrow this chapter as a mood. It is a mechanism. The mechanism is unkind to steering.

In the middle — which is where we live — chaos is why forecasts have horizons. We can reconstruct the three minutes because the soup was mixed and the laws were simple. We cannot forecast a galaxy’s weather in year 10¹². We can forecast the stretch. The stretch, with dark energy in the budget, looks dull and huge. Dull and huge is a kind of mercy. The web freezes in place as the shove between filaments wins. Galaxies go dark on their own clocks. The engine that built them idles.

Weather versus stretch is the distinction worth taking home. Weather is Lyapunov’s child: a breath here, a different storm next week. The stretch is not that kind of forecast. The Friedmann equations, fed a budget of pull and shove, do not forget their nearby starts on a weather clock. They tell you the dough will keep rising, and, with the shove in the account, rising faster. You can be wrong about the second digit of the Hubble number and still be right that the voids will get ruder. You cannot be a little wrong about a hurricane’s seed and still be right about next Tuesday. Do not confuse the two clocks. One is why you pack an umbrella. The other is why the cosmic web is a fossil in progress.

A kitchen radio that promised you Tuesday’s rain from Monday’s steam would be lying by Wednesday if the Lyapunov time had already run out. A cosmology that promised you the leftover glow from the three minutes is not lying: the soup was mixed, the laws were simple, the clock was short and hot. A cosmology that promised you the weather inside a particular spiral in year 10¹² would be the radio. The stretch forecast is closer to the soup than to the rain. Dull and huge, as the earlier paragraph said. Mercy, if you like mercy. A fossil, if you like fossils. The web we map with SDSS and 2dF is the baking caught mid-loaf. The shove will finish the bake by stretching the tins apart until no new filaments form. That end of the engine is Chapter 18’s personality change. This chapter’s personality is the engine itself.

Barrow would have called the Omega Point an idea for ideas and then, if he were in a kind mood, walked you to the mixmaster and let the mixer do the unkindness. Chapter 19 will do the data. Here the mixer is enough: crunch chaos is a second lock on that door. Nearby starts, unrecognizable rooms. No steering wheel survives the last decimals.

Keep chaos in the book as a tool, not a vibe. It made the rooms. It will not let anyone own the ending. It is the opposite of a watchmaker and the opposite of a destiny. It is what a twitch can do when the universe gives it time.

The dropped tray is still on the floor. Same ingredients. Different loaves. You live in one of them. The leftover glow is the photograph of the spill before the baking. The survey map is the photograph after. In between, gravity did what gravity does with a nick of size 10⁻⁵ and a lot of idle hours. That is an engine. It does not owe you a particular loaf. It owes you the family: webs, not destiny; sensitivity, not mood; a mixer at the far end that nobody steers.

If you want a last kitchen instruction: pick up the tray. Notice that picking it up does not unspill the yeast. The web is already baked into the surveys. The leftover glow is already on the antenna. Chaos, as an engine, has already done the morning’s work. What it will not do, tonight or in a crunch, is hand you a recipe that steers the last decimal. Barrow’s pebble, Guth’s unpaid bills, Mukhanov’s twitch, Linde’s next-room pot, and this dropped tray are one family of honesties. The universe is sensitive. It is not a mood. It is how rooms get made, and why nobody owns the ending.

\newpage

# PART IV — The Missing Most

---

\newpage

## 17. The Extra Pull

Dark matter is a terrible name. It sounds like a feeling, or a brand of chocolate, or the opposite of enlightenment. It is none of those. It is a pull we can weigh and cannot photograph in ordinary light.

Barrow, in the short origin book this one uses as a model, had a habit of refusing a pretty noun until a measurement had earned it. Dark matter has not earned a pretty noun. It has earned a scale. The scale is the extra weight in galaxies and in clusters: more pull than the shining stars and gas can account for, by a factor of several. The name is a confession. We weighed it. We have not bagged it.

In the kitchen you do not weigh a truck by climbing into the trailer and counting crates. You weigh it by how the bridge leans when the truck rolls on — by the strain gauges, the sag, the way the other traffic feels the extra. Dark matter is the trailer you cannot open. The bridge still leans. That is the whole method of this chapter, and it is why the extra pull is a hot fact even while the particle is a warm rumor.

In the 1930s, Fritz Zwicky timed the galaxies in the Coma Cluster and found them moving too fast. The visible stars did not supply enough gravity to hold the swarm. He said so, rudely, and was mostly ignored. In the 1970s, Vera Rubin and Kent Ford timed stars in the disks of spiral galaxies and found the same insult at a friendlier scale: stars out in the suburbs orbit as fast as stars closer in, as if most of the mass were in a big, dark halo, not in the shining bulge. The light is a liar about the weight.

The Coma paper is 1933, and the cluster is a city of cities: thousands of galaxies in a well so deep that, if you time them as Zwicky did, the virial theorem becomes a speed trap. If the members are this fast, the well has to be this deep, or the swarm would have flown apart long ago. The well was too deep for the starlight. The rudeness was not the problem. The problem was that nobody wanted an extra ingredient that did not shine. Extra ingredients are expensive. Astronomy in the 1930s was still finishing the argument about whether spiral nebulae were even other galaxies. An invisible majority was a second insult.

Rubin and Ford brought the second instrument down to a single disk. A rotation curve is speed versus distance from the center, a plot that Newtonian common sense said should fall once you leave the shining bulge. Their curves stayed flat. Flat is not a mood. Flat is *v* staying large while *r* grows, which, if gravity is still gravity, means *M* is still growing in the dark. They did this galaxy after galaxy, with a spectrograph and a stubbornness that later textbooks still under-thank. The suburban stars were clocks. The clocks did not slow.

What dark matter is *not*, while we are cleaning the noun:

It is not dark energy. Dark energy shoves. Dark matter pulls. They share a marketing department and nothing else.

Keep the divorce or the rest of this part will lie to you. The extra pull clumps. It makes halos. The extra shove does none of those things. Chapter 18 is the other spouse. Do not let a shared adjective fuse two ingredients.

It is not a black hole in the everyday sense, though a zoo of old, small black holes is a cold candidate people still kick. Ordinary black holes made from dead stars are not numerous enough, and we have looked.

Microlensing surveys have walked the Magellanic Clouds looking for the wink of a compact object passing in front of a star. They did not find a halo made of dead stars. A halo made of primordial pebbles of collapsed spacetime is still allowed by some papers and disliked by others. It is not the working fluid.

It is not the failure of our eyes. We have looked in radio, infrared, X-ray. The missing weight does not glow in those either, not as atoms would.

Atoms, given a chance, find a wavelength. The extra pull has had thirteen billion years of chances and has not found one we can tune a dish to. Hidden steam would have shown up as heat, as lines, as a fog in some band.

It is not a mood in the equations. The leftover glow’s freckles have a pattern that, when grown by gravity, matches a universe in which most of the pulling-stuff does not collide with light. Atoms slam into photons in the early plasma and leave a different signature. The extra pull did not slam. It is *non-baryonic* in the jargon: not the protons-and-neutrons club. Appendix A17 says that more carefully. Here: it is a different ingredient, not hidden steam.

The leftover helium of Chapter 10 already counted the baryons. There are not enough of them. You cannot hide the extra weight in more hydrogen that forgot to shine. The nuclear minutes already took inventory. The leftover glow’s odd and even acoustic peaks take the same inventory a second way. Two fossils, two methods, one minority of atoms. The extra pull is the difference between that minority and the weight the clocks and the arcs demand.

Candidates have names that sound like card games. WIMPs: heavy, shy particles that would leave a rare cough in an underground tank. Axions: wisps from an old fix to nuclear physics, convertible into photons in a magnetic field if you listen with a sufficiently obsessive radio. Sterile neutrinos: a shy cousin of the neutrinos we already know. None of them has been caught in the act. The tanks have gotten very quiet. XENON, a vat of noble liquid under a mountain in Italy, listens for WIMP coughs and hears the Earth’s radioactivity and the Sun’s neutrinos and not much else. LZ, a larger vat in a South Dakota mine, does the same with a stiller ear. The radios have gotten very proud. The sky has not changed its mind about the pull.

WIMPs were the easy hope: weakly interacting massive particles, heavy enough to sit still on galaxy scales, shy enough to pass through a mountain, common enough that a ton of liquid xenon should, over a year, record a few recoils. The year keeps ending without the recoils. Axions were a patch Peccei and Quinn wrote for a problem in the strong force; if they exist at the right mass they can be the halo, and a magnetic cavity can try to turn them into a microwave whisper. The whisper has not arrived. Sterile neutrinos are a shy extra flavor that would not join the usual weak interactions; some recipes make them cold enough to scaffold galaxies, some make them warm enough to erase dwarf galaxies we can still see. None of the three has been caught in the act. That silence is a scientific result, not a failure of nerve. A WIMP at the mass and interaction strength that would have made the tanks cough by now is, to a first approximation, not there. The window has not slammed shut on every mass. It has slammed shut on the masses that were supposed to be easy.

Modified gravity — change the law instead of adding an ingredient — still has clever friends. It has a harder time with clusters, with the leftover glow, and with the Bullet, where two kinds of stuff have been caught in the act of *not staying together*. The working theory is an extra ingredient. The working theory has a hole in it the size of a noun. That is allowed. Chemistry once had a hole named “what is fire.” The hole was not a reason to deny the flame.

MOND, and the relativistic clothes it has tried on, can mimic a flat rotation curve in a lonely disk with a tweak to Newton at low accelerations. A lonely disk is not a colliding pair of clusters. If gravity itself were simply stronger on large scales, the slammed gas and the extra weight would still sit on the same map, because they would be the same map. They do not. Some papers try to dress modified gravity so the offset still happens. The try is allowed. The try has not become the working theory. Barrow would have walked you to the photograph, named the extra ingredient, and then said the noun is still an idea for a laboratory, not a trophy.

How do you weigh something that refuses to shine?

You watch what it does to things that do. Galaxies lean. Clusters lean. The leftover glow rang like a bell, and bells remember what was pulling on them.

Rotation curves are the suburban clocks: stars and gas, timed in their orbits, going too fast. Gravitational lensing is the more theatrical trick. A cluster’s weight bends spacetime, and the bend makes multiple, stretched images of galaxies behind it — arcs, crosses, rings. Count the arcs, measure the stretch, and you have a scale. The scale disagrees with the starlight by a factor of several. The disagreement is the extra pull.

Weak lensing, which does not make a full arc, still shears the background into a statistical lean. Stack enough galaxies and the lean is a map. Strong lensing is the theater. Weak lensing is the survey. Both weigh the trailer by the bridge. Neither needs the crates to glow.

The leftover glow offers a third scale. The freckles are a sound. The sound has harmonics, like a bell. The position and height of those harmonics depend on how much ordinary matter was slamming into light, and how much quiet matter was only pulling. The bell says: a little slamming stuff, a lot of quiet stuff. The numbers line up with the clocks and the arcs. Three instruments, one extra weight.

Before atoms formed, the plasma could ring: photons and baryons slamming, compressing, bouncing, while the extra pull sat in the wells and did not slam. Odd peaks versus even peaks are a baryon census. Peak positions are a total-matter census. A single method can lie. Three methods, of different families, lying in the same direction, is a courtroom. That is why this chapter’s first sentence is hot even though the last noun is missing.

Then there is the Bullet.

The catalog name is 1E 0657-56. In 2006, Clowe and colleagues published the map that made the argument visual even for people who dislike maps.

Two clusters have passed through each other. The gas — atoms, shining in X-rays — slammed, shocked, and lagged in the middle, the way two crowds trying to walk through each other get stuck at the doors. The extra pull, mapped by lensing, did not slam. It went on through, like two ghosts politely declining to notice they had overlapped. In the famous color posters, gas is pink and mass is blue. On a Kindle those posters lie. Figure 17 is one composite: the slammed gas labeled where it piled up, the mass labeled where lensing put it, same scene. The contours do not sit on the gas. That is the photograph of “not atoms.”

If you wanted modified gravity to swallow the extra pull, this is the exhibit it has to explain without an extra ingredient that can leave the gas behind. The gas is most of the shining baryons in the cluster. After the crossing, the gas is in the middle. The lensing mass is not. Collisionless stuff goes on through. Collisional stuff sticks.

![Figure 17. The Bullet Cluster as one composite: hot gas piled in the middle; mass, mapped by lensing, on either side. The pull did not stay with the gas.](Figures/figs/fig17.jpg)

Hot claim: there is more pulling-stuff than shining-stuff, and the extra stuff does not collide the way gas does. Warm claim: it is a particle we have not bagged, or a small zoo of them. Cold claim: we know its name, its mass, and which underground tank will cough first. We do not.

The extra pull is why galaxies are not just pretty. It is the scaffolding they grew on. Without the quiet halos, the twitches of Chapter 14 would have had a harder time becoming spirals before the stretch pulled the scaffolding out of reach. You live in a suburb of a ghost.

The baryons, left to themselves, would have been a thinner foam. The quiet wells were already there, grown from the same freckles, and the gas fell in. The scaffolding is not a purpose. It is a head start. Gravity ran on noise in the dark wells first; the shining disks are late tenants. The ghost is an ingredient, not a destiny, and not a synonym for the shove. It owes you a weight. The weight is on the bridge. The trailer is still locked.

---

\newpage

## 18. The Shove

In 1998 two teams of astronomers, racing each other with dying stars, found that the stretch is not sulking. It is speeding up.

Saul Perlmutter’s Supernova Cosmology Project, and the High-Z team of Adam Riess and Brian Schmidt, were doing the same rude job: hunting Type Ia supernovae far enough away that their light had been in transit for billions of years, and asking whether the stretch had been tiring the way gravity said it should. Two teams, two pipelines, one sky. That is how you keep a shared mistake from dressing up as a discovery. In 2011 the three of them shared a Nobel Prize for the answer. The answer was no. The stretch had been getting a second wind.

The dying stars were Type Ia supernovae, a kind of standard bomb: a white dwarf that reaches a bad weight and detonates with a brightness you can almost trust. Trust the brightness, measure how faint it looks, and you have distance. Measure the stretch of its light and you have the universe’s size when it exploded. Plot size against time for bombs going off over the last several billion years and you expected, if you were educated before 1998, a slowdown. Gravity pulls. Stretching should tire.

The bombs were too faint. They were farther than the tired-stretch story allowed. The stretch had *accelerated*. Something was shoving.

Acceleration, in the kitchen, is not a galaxy putting its foot down. A raisin does not gun an engine. The dough between raisins changes its mind about how fast to rise. The plot of distance against stretch, once it is corrected for the way candles look faint, tips the wrong way for a tired universe. Gravity is still pulling inside the raisins. Between them, the budget has a term that does not thin, and that term has started to win.

A Type Ia is not a random firework. It is a white dwarf — a dead star’s leftover ember, about Earth’s size and the Sun’s mass — stealing matter from a companion, or merging with another dwarf, until it crosses a mass where electron degeneracy can no longer hold it. The explosion is nuclear and brief and, after you calibrate the way the light fades, nearly the same candle each time. Nearly is doing a lot of work. The teams spent years arguing about dust, about evolution, about whether a candle at redshift one is still the candle you calibrated nearby. The argument did not eat the result. Later candles, better calibrated, still sit too far away. That is how a hot claim is born: not from a press conference, from a result that survives the people who wanted it to be dust.

The simplest shove is a cosmological constant: an energy that comes with empty space itself, a tension Einstein once put in to freeze the universe and then regretted. The vacuum, in quantum field theory, is allowed to have a cost. The cost it *should* have, if you naively add up the jitters of every field down to the Planck scale, is off by a number so large it has become a joke: 10¹²⁰, give or take a few zeros. The measured shove is tiny. The predicted vacuum is a monster. This is the worst prediction in physics if you are in the mood to be rude, and one of the deepest problems if you are not.

Einstein wanted a static loaf. The constant was a fudge to hold the raisins still. When Hubble’s stretch showed the raisins were not still, he called the fudge his blunder. The sky, seventy years later, hired the blunder back — not to freeze the loaf, but to speed the later rise. Barrow liked numbers that make a kitchen feel small. This one makes the kitchen feel like a rounding error that somehow landed on a habitable decimal. If the vacuum energy were even a few orders of magnitude larger, galaxies would not have had time to assemble before the shove tore the wells apart. Weinberg said that out loud in 1987, as an anthropic upper bound, before the bombs had voted. The bombs voted near the bound. That is either a clue about an ensemble of vacuums — Chapter 15’s rumor — or a coincidence we have not earned the right to spend. Warm either way. Not a photograph.

Dark energy is the placeholder name for the shove. It might be Einstein’s constant. It might be a field that changes slowly, a “quintessence,” which is a word that sounds like a perfume and behaves like a maybe. It might be a mistake in how we read distances. The last option has been losing for twenty-five years. The leftover glow, the baryon acoustic oscillations — a leftover sound-scale printed in the way galaxies cluster — and the bombs now tell the same story. About two-thirds of the budget is the shove.

Quintessence would have an equation of state *w* that can wander a little. Einstein’s constant sits at *w = −1* and does not wander. The data, as of the kitchen date of this book, sit at *w ≈ −1*. That is a measured shrug in the direction of the constant, not a proof that the perfume is empty. A *w* that later turns around and becomes a crunch is still a cold loophole. It is not the working fit. The working fit is a shove that looks, to the present instruments, like empty space with a tiny, stubborn cost.

The sound-scale is worth a kitchen minute. In the plasma of Chapter 9, baryons and photons rang together. When the fog lifted, the ringing froze. The frozen wavelength is a ruler about 150 megaparsecs long, comoving — a stick you can still lay across the clustering of galaxies today. That is BAO: baryon acoustic oscillations. It is not a bomb. It is a leftover chime. Lay the ruler at different redshifts and you measure how the stretch has been behaving, *H(z)*, the Hubble rate as a function of how much the universe has grown. Appendix A18 writes the Friedmann line for the case *w = −1*:

*H²(z)/H₀² = Ω_m (1+z)³ + Ω_r (1+z)⁴ + Ω_Λ + Ω_k (1+z)²*

Matter thins as the cube of the stretch. Radiation thins as the fourth power. A cosmological constant does not thin. That is the whole plot twist in one line: at late times the thing that does not thin takes over, and the stretch accelerates. Acceleration, in the data, begins around redshift 0.6 — a few billion years ago. The last part of the bake is the fast part.

It does not clump. It does not make halos. It does not sit in the Bullet. It is not dark matter in a hat. If you take nothing else from this chapter, take the divorce: pull and shove are not a couple.

The extra pull is scaffolding. The extra shove is a later wind between the scaffolds. You can live in a suburb of a ghost and still be carried apart from the next suburb by a dough that has started to hurry. The marketing department will keep printing “dark” on both bottles. The kitchen will keep two spoons.

Once the shove is in the budget, the future changes personality.

Without it, you still had a fight between pull and stretch. Enough atoms-and-ghosts and the universe might someday stop expanding and fall back. Too little and it would stretch forever, tiring, thinning, cooling. The knife-edge of Chapter 12 was, in part, a knife-edge of fate.

With the shove, the fight is already over, unless the shove itself is a liar that will someday switch sides. The stretch accelerates. Isolated lumps — galaxies, bound clusters — remain bound. Between them, space wins and keeps winning. The cosmic web freezes. New filaments stop forming. The voids get ruder.

A second-digit fight has been making the newspapers, and it should not be allowed to steal the first digit. Local candles and nearby geometry — Cepheids, nearby Type Ia, a geometric trick with water masers — want a Hubble constant a little larger than the leftover glow prefers, once the glow is grown forward with the working model. Planck’s sky, read through the shove and the extra pull, likes a slope near 67 kilometers per second per megaparsec. Some local ladders like 73. That is the Hubble tension. It is a real argument among careful people. It is an argument about the slope *now*, about whether we have misread a candle or missed a late-time wrinkle. It is not an argument that the stretch is not accelerating. Both camps still need a shove. The bombs are still too faint. The 150-megaparsec ruler still says the late bake sped up. Do not let a fight about the second digit become a rumor that 1998 did not happen.

![Figure 18. The Crab Nebula, a supernova remnant with a bright rim. Explosions of a related kind, seen much farther off, taught us the stretch is speeding up.](Figures/figs/fig18.jpg)

Appendix A18 writes *H(z)* and the distance-redshift relation. Here is the kitchen version. Imagine a raisin bread that not only rises but starts rising faster in the last part of the bake. The raisins that are already touching stay a family. The raisins that were only neighbors become, in time, rumors. The early bake was the pull’s era: matter thinning, stretch tiring. The last bake is the shove’s. You can feel that as a change in the dough without ever naming a field. *H(z)* is only the baker’s clock for how fast the pan is growing at each stage.

That is bound versus unbound in a loaf whose yeast has found a second wind. You, in a galaxy, are inside a raisin. The next cluster over may still be family. The next supercluster is already practicing being a rumor. The kitchen mistake is to think the raisins themselves are swelling. Bound things hold. The dough between the unbound is what hurries.

This is why a required Omega Point — a final crunch in which life steers the universe and computes forever — fails as physics, not as poetry. Poetry can crunch whatever it wants. The bombs, the leftover glow, and the frozen sound-scale say we are not on a crunch track. The next pages put shoes on that sentence.

A detail for later travel: the accelerating stretch makes a *cosmic event horizon*. There are galaxies whose light, emitted today, will never reach us, and there are galaxies we now see whose future light never will. Most of the universe has already left, not by walking away, but by the dough rising too well. Chapter 26 is the map. Chapter 18 is why the map is not optional.

A horizon of this kind is not a black hole. It is a fact about which events can still be connected by light in a loaf that keeps hurrying. The kitchen clock cannot hold it. The kitchen clock never could. If the shove stays near *w ≈ −1*, the map gets ruder with time: more of the web leaves the set of events that can still answer a signal. That is not despair. It is a reason to keep the word *now* local, and a reason Chapter 26 has a job.

Hot claim: the expansion is accelerating. Warm claim: a cosmological constant, or something that looks like one, is the shove. Cold claim: we know why the shove is the size it is. We do not. The worst prediction in physics is still on the board.

---

\newpage

## 19. Why Forever-Mind Does Not Follow

Frank Tipler, working from a book he wrote with Barrow, described a final state in which the universe recollapses, intelligent information-processing takes the controls, the amount of computation diverges, and this last, infinitely clever moment is — he was not shy — God. He called it the Omega Point. He said the known laws require it. He said life must never die out.

The book was *The Anthropic Cosmological Principle*, 1986, a thick, serious, sometimes dazzling inventory of how the constants look arranged, followed by a last act that stopped being an inventory. Barrow’s name is on the cover. Barrow’s later habit, in the short origin book, was the one this chapter will keep: walk you up to a speculation and then call it an idea for ideas. Tipler kept walking. He later wrote a thicker popularization in which the last computation wore the noun more openly. The Omega Point is what you get if you let an idea for ideas put on a lab coat and then a mitre.

The known laws do not require it. The data have been voting no since 1998.

A crunch needs the pull to win. The shove is winning. For the Omega Point to be a destiny rather than a story, the shove would have to fade, reverse, or be a mirage. None of those is the working theory. A story can still imagine a different bubble, a different branch, a later surprise in the vacuum. A destiny cannot hide in a surprise. Destiny is supposed to be what the laws demand. The laws, plus the bombs — the Type Ia supernovae of Chapter 18, not weapons — demand a long, thinning, accelerating future, not a steered collapse.

Here is the kitchen version, and it is ruder than a diagram. Imagine a will that names destinies the bank account cannot pay. The will is eloquent. It leaves the house to a dynasty, the orchard to a school, the future to a mind that never sleeps. Then you open the ledger. The shove is two-thirds of the budget. Recollapse is not in the data. The will is still a document. The bank is still empty of that particular future. You do not get to call the will a law of physics because the prose was confident.

Perlmutter, Riess, and Schmidt did not set out to probate a metaphysical estate. They set out to weigh the stretch. The estate failed anyway. A required forever-mind needed the loaf to turn over. The loaf tipped up. Twenty-five years of leftover glow, of the 150-megaparsec ruler, of later candles, have not put recollapse back on the working track. Warm loopholes remain — a *w* that turns around, a vacuum that decays, a different bubble. Spend those as destinies and you have left the data for a story. Chapter 18 already said the story is allowed. Destiny is not the word for a loophole.

Barrow himself, in the short origin book this one uses as a formal model, had a better habit: he would walk you up to a speculation and then call it an idea for ideas. The Final Anthropic Principle — intelligent processing must arise and must never die out — is an idea for ideas that put on a lab coat. Martin Gardner made a joke of the acronym. The joke was unkind. The data have been unkinder.

In the 1986 book the Final Anthropic Principle is stated as a requirement, not a hope: observers come into existence, and once they do, they persist. That is FAP. Gardner, a working skeptic with a magazine column and no patience for cosmic last acts dressed as theorems, suggested that a principle which *demands* eternal observers deserved a cruder expansion of the letters. He did not need the 1998 bombs. He needed only the difference between a condition we observe from — we are here, therefore the universe is the kind of place that can have a here — and a commandment that we must remain. The first is the weak anthropic cut, a likelihood filter, useful once you have an ensemble. The second is a wish with a Latin name. Chapter 41 will give the filter its honest job. Here the job is simpler: FAP is not selected by the leftover glow, the leftover helium, the extra pull, or the extra shove. It is selected by a desire that the story not end without a speaker.

![Figure 19. Three curves in three line styles: recollapse, coast, accelerate. The one marked data is the accelerating curve.](Figures/figs/fig19.png)

Look at the figure until it is boring. Recollapse is a curve that turns over. Coast is a curve that tires. Accelerate is a curve that tips up. The data sit on the one that tips up. Appendix A19 writes the same sentence with density parameters: observed matter about 0.3, observed shove about 0.7, deceleration parameter negative. Closed, matter-only recollapse needed a universe heavier than the critical density and no lasting shove. We are not that universe. The working fit is the accelerating curve.

Barrow would have made you sit with the figure before he let you talk about God. Three line styles, one of them marked data. The temptation is to treat the other two as equally live options, the way a menu treats three dinners. They are not equally live. Recollapse was the dinner the 1986 last act needed. The kitchen is not serving it. An idea for ideas may still draw the turning-over curve. A destiny may not.

Chaos, from Chapter 16, is the second objection, even in a universe that *did* crunch. Near a final singularity — the same wall Chapter 16 named and Chapter 24 properly explains — the mixmaster scrambles. Steering requires control. Control requires that nearby futures stay nearby. They do not. Infinite computation in a dying crunch is a wish that the geometry be a computer. Geometry is not that polite.

This is the objection that would survive a miracle. Suppose, against the bombs, the shove later faded and the loaf turned over. Suppose a civilization of perfect machines was ready with a plan. The plan still has to steer a geometry that, near the end, swaps its axes the way a chaotic kitchen fire swaps which pan is hottest. You cannot cook a required last thought on a stove that will not hold a setting. FAP said life must never die out. BKL says that even a dying crunch is not a stove you can borrow. The first objection is the data. The second is the shape of Einstein’s ending, crunch or no crunch.

Belinski, Khalatnikov, and Lifshitz — BKL, in the jargon — showed that a generic spacelike crunch in Einstein’s theory does not approach a polite point. It approaches a sequence of Kasner eras: squash in two directions, stretch in one, then swap, then swap again, the axes changing in a way that is chaotic. Barrow proved things in that vicinity about how little control you get. Say that again without the names, because the names can sound like a priestly veto. If the loaf recollapses, the last slice is not a quiet room in which a civilization of perfect machines can store more and more bits in less and less space. The last slice is a scramble. Tiny differences become different universes of shape. A steered crunch to a single, infinitely processed point is a measure-zero demand unless you add extra structure by hand. Extra structure by hand is not “the known laws require it.” Extra structure by hand is a story.

Tipler needed a recollapse, a cosmology that could be forced toward a point, a way to dump unlimited energy into unlimited computation, and a reading of God as that last computation. Each of those is a cold brick. Stacked, they are a chapel. This book will not bless the chapel. It will also not sneer at the hunger that built it. People want the story to have a speaker at the end. The leftover glow does not hire speakers. The Bekenstein count of Chapter 23 will later make the bit-budget ruder still: shelf space likes area, not a vanishing volume asked to hold an infinity. The first objection does not need that count. The first objection is the shove.

None of this says mind is small. It says mind is not a clause in the gravitational field equation. If we want mind to last — in machines that do not sleep, in libraries of Earth sent to dark seas under ice, in a greenhouse on a dead world — that is a project. Projects can fail. Destinies, if they are real, cannot. The distinction is the moral of this book’s second half. Meaning, if you want it, is local, expensive, and optional. The leftover helium does not care. The leftover glow does not care. The shove does not care. That is why a greenhouse is interesting. It is a care that the universe did not budget for.

The greenhouse is a thought with dirt on it: someone, somewhere, keeping a volume of air at a temperature a plant can use, on a world the shove and the pull did not arrange for gardening. The hours are still hours. The failure modes are still failure modes. The universe did not set aside a line item. The line item, if it exists, is us, locally, for a while. That is not a blessing of forever-mind. It is the opposite. Forever-mind was the claim that the field equation would staff the garden. The field equation staffs a stretch. The garden, if it happens, is overtime.

Meaning, in this book, is that overtime. It is not a clause that appears when the scale factor is large. It is a woman checking a valve, a library that can fail in a fire, a melt-probe that can miss the sea. The leftover helium will still be a quarter by mass if the valve sticks. The shove will still be two-thirds of the budget if the library burns. That is not a scolding. It is why the care is interesting. Care that the cosmos already budgeted for would be weather. Care it did not budget for is a project.

A will that names a forever-mind is the same document as FAP in a more theatrical font. The bank account is the energy budget and the stretch. The bank will pay galaxies that stay bound, stars that finish their fuel, holes that leak on clocks we will not live to read, a leftover glow that thins, a web that freezes. It will not pay a required last computation. You may still write libraries. You may still staff a greenhouse. You may still send a melt-probe into a dark sea. Those are checks you write on a local account. They clear, or they bounce, in kitchens. They do not clear because the cosmos owes you a dynasty.

Hot claim: the working cosmology is not a recollapse. The bombs, the leftover glow, and the frozen ruler say the stretch is accelerating. Warm claim: the shove will keep behaving like a cosmological constant, *w ≈ −1*, long enough that the web freezes and most worldlines never meet again. Cold claim: mind is scheduled to take the controls and compute without end. That claim is not selected by the data. It is a will the ledger cannot honor.

Forever-mind does not follow. What follows, if you want a moral from a budget, is smaller and ruder and better: meaning is a local project, or it is not a project at all. The universe did not budget for it. That is the permission. It is also the bill.

\newpage

# PART V — One-Way Membranes

---

\newpage

## 20. A Horizon Is a Fact About Events

A black hole is not a cosmic drain with a personality. It is a one-way membrane in the loaf.

Pick an event — a flash of light, a last radio beep, the moment a clock’s hand ticks. From that event, some future events can still be reached by a signal that never exceeds light. Some cannot. The boundary between those two sets, when gravity has bent the loaf enough, can close on itself. Inside, every future of every event leads deeper. Outside, you can still choose. The closed boundary is the horizon. It is not made of a material. It is made of *facts about which events can influence which others*.

That sentence is the whole chapter, if you let it sit. A horizon is not a black wall, not a hungry mouth, not a place you could chip with a hammer. It is a sorting of the loaf. Chapter 3 already gave you the tool: from any event, a future light cone of things you can still affect and a past cone of things that can still affect you. In ordinary space those cones stay open. Near a sufficiently compact mass they tip and close. Cross the closed surface and Earth’s worldline is no longer in your future cone. You can still be in Earth’s past cone for a while — as a signal, as a photograph, as a last beep. The asymmetry is not a mood. It is geometry.

This is a different object from the *cosmic* event horizon of Chapter 26. That one is a fact about the whole loaf’s later stretch: galaxies whose light, emitted today, never arrives. This one is local. A star collapses. A membrane forms around a clump. Most of the universe does not notice. Do not blur the two just because both are called horizons. One is a weather of the expanding dough. One is a fact about a compact mass.

We knew the local kind was real before anyone made a picture of a shadow. In the 1960s and 1970s, X-ray telescopes found binaries in which a visible star whipped around an unseen compact partner too heavy to be a neutron star. Cygnus X-1 was the rude prototype: a blue giant, a dark companion of more than a few Suns, gas screaming in X-rays as it lost an argument with an orbit it could not leave. The case was circumstantial and then it was not. No other object that heavy and that small holds itself up. The membrane was inferred the way a well is inferred from a missing bucket and a splash you cannot retrieve. The later pictures did not invent the well. They stopped the argument about whether the splash was a mood.

Karl Schwarzschild, in 1916, found the first exact hole in Einstein’s equations while dying, more or less, on the Eastern Front. He was an artilleryman as well as a mathematician. Between trajectory tables he took Einstein’s new field equations, assumed a mass that did not spin and did not charge, and found a clean solution Einstein had not yet written down. He posted the result to Berlin. Einstein read it to the Prussian Academy. Schwarzschild was dead by May, of an illness the war had not invented but had not helped. The radius that now carries his name is

*r_s = 2GM / c²*

*where G is Newton’s constant, M is the mass, and c is the speed of light.*

The formula is not a decoration. It says how small you must squeeze a mass before the membrane forms. Double the mass, double the radius. The speed of light sits in the denominator as a square, which is why everyday objects have absurdly tiny horizons and stars have horizons you could, in a dark mood, walk across. Appendix A20 writes the line and notes that the horizon is a coordinate scare, not a curvature scare: invariants stay finite there. The singularity, later, is the place the theory actually breaks.

A fact about events can still have a radius. That is the useful shock. The sorting of cones happens at a place you can write in kilometers, or millimeters, once you know the mass. For a stellar hole of ten Suns the radius is about thirty kilometers — a city you could hide under a cloud. For Sagittarius A* it is about twelve million kilometers, a good fraction of Mercury’s reach. For M87* it is tens of billions of kilometers, a solar system of darkness. None of those numbers is a wall. Each is the distance, in Schwarzschild’s labels, at which “you can still send a letter home” stops being true. The labels misbehave at that surface; the facts do not. Light aimed outward from just inside never arrives. Light aimed outward from just outside can, if it is lucky and not circling the photon sphere forever.

For the Sun, that radius is about three kilometers. The Sun is not a black hole; it is much larger than its Schwarzschild radius, and proud of it. Squeeze the Sun into those three kilometers and the membrane forms. For Earth, the radius is about nine millimeters — a pea, not a city. For a mountain, it is smaller than a nucleus and we do not live in a cartoon. Nature does not squeeze mountains. Nature squeezes the cores of heavy stars. When the core can no longer hold, and the leftover is heavier than the two or three Suns a neutron star can bear, the membrane is not optional. It is what the equations do with that much mass in that little room.

For the object in the middle of the galaxy M87, the radius is comparable to a solar system. Six and a half billion Suns, packed so that the one-way surface is tens of billions of kilometers across. The Event Horizon Telescope did not photograph the membrane. It photographed the *shadow*: the silhouette of paths that could not escape, against a glowing ring of gas that still could. The picture was released in 2019. It is a radio mosaic from a planet-sized interferometer, not a snapshot from a tourist bus. In 2022 the same collaboration published the hole in our own cellar — Sagittarius A*, four million Suns, a horizon the size of a planetary orbit, flickering on the timescale of a coffee break. Andrea Ghez and Reinhard Genzel had already timed the stars that whip around that cellar, and taken a Nobel for the timing. The shadow was the picture that matched the clocks.

![Figure 20. The ring and dark center of M87*, enlarged. The shadow of a one-way surface.](Figures/figs/fig20.jpg)

A woman at a console in 2019 — invented, as the How to Read promised; the ring is not — watched the press image load and said, to no one, “That’s a hole.” She was almost right. It is the *absence* of paths. Photons that grazed the photon sphere, a circle at one and a half Schwarzschild radii for a hole that does not spin, can loop and still crawl out to a telescope. Photons that aimed closer cannot. The dark patch is a census of failed escapes. The bright ring is a census of last chances. The membrane itself is not in the JPEG. It is a fact about events the JPEG is allowed to imply.

Hot claim: horizons of this kind exist. Stars orbit something compact and enormous in galactic centers. Mergers ring spacetime and LIGO hears the ring. The shadow is now a picture. On 14 September 2015 — a Monday, if you like kitchen calendars — a ripple passed through Hanford and Livingston, a few milliseconds apart, the way a fact about two colliding holes should. The event is called GW150914. Two holes, about thirty-six and twenty-nine Suns, finished a dance 1.3 billion light-years from this kitchen and became one hole of about sixty-two Suns. Three Suns’ worth of mass left as gravitational waves. For an instant the glow in those waves outshone, in raw power, every star we can name. The announcement came in February 2016. Since then the catalog has grown: more stellar holes, neutron-star pairs, a zoo of masses no optical telescope had weighed. The ringdown is the loaf settling after a slap. It is as close as we have come to hearing a horizon be a horizon.

Warm claim: inside the membrane, Einstein’s theory keeps going until a singularity, a place where the theory itself throws up its hands. The classical interior is a prediction of the same equations that got the shadow and the ringdown right. It is not a photograph. Cold claim: we know what replaces the singularity. We do not. Quantum gravity is a research program. Loop sketches try to bounce. String theory offers replacements — fuzz, a tangle of extra dimensions, a horizon that is not quite a horizon. **String theory cannot currently be tested.** A replacement that cannot be weighed is an idea for ideas, in Barrow’s honest sense, not a second picture to hang beside Figure 20.

The kitchen mistake is to think of the hole as an object you could poke. The useful thought is the membrane of events.

Imagine a last radio beep. A probe, invented, falls toward a stellar hole while a ship waits far out. The probe’s beacon is a metronome. At first the ship hears it on schedule, Doppler and all. Then the ticks stretch. Each beep arrives redder, then redder still, as if someone were pulling the wavelength the way Chapter 8 pulls galaxy light. The probe, by its own clock, crosses the membrane in finite proper time and keeps falling. The ship never quite receives a last tick. The signal fades, redshifts, and is lost in the noise. Earth can still include the probe in *its* past, for a while, as a redder and redder last beep. The probe’s future cone no longer includes Earth. That asymmetry is the whole joke and the whole tragedy. It is also why a hole is not a door.

A woman on the ship — invented; the redshift is not — sits with headphones and a log. She marks the frequency of each beep. 1.4 gigahertz. Then 1.2. Then a smear she has to argue with the noise about. She is not watching a drain swallow a box. She is watching a sequence of events lose the right to be heard. The probe’s last second, on the probe’s clock, still happens. It is a crumb in the loaf. It is not a crumb the ship is allowed to share as a contemporaneous tick. “Now,” which was already in trouble in the kitchen, has no job left at the membrane. There is the probe’s proper time, finite and doomed. There is the ship’s proper time, in which the last beep is an ever-redder rumor. There is no third clock that declares them simultaneous.

A drain in a kitchen sink is a poor metaphor and a useful one. Water goes down. You do not get the water back by staring at the hole. But a drain is a material, with a rim you can touch, and a plumber can reverse it with a snake. A horizon has no rim you can chip. There is no snake. The “down” is a fact about cones. Cross it and you have not entered a basement. You have left the set of events that can still send a letter home.

Barrow liked to shock readers with a number that fits in a teaspoon. Earth’s horizon is nine millimeters. The entire planet, squeezed to a pea, would wear a membrane you could hide under a thumb. We do not do that. Gravity, given a few dozen Suns in a collapsing core, does. The shock is not the cartoon. The shock is that the same formula that makes a pea out of Earth makes a solar-system-sized shadow out of M87*, and that both numbers are now, in different ways, measured.

So: a horizon is a fact about events. It is not a personality, not a drain, not a door. The next chapter parks a clock beside one and watches “now” come apart in a more intimate way. Chapter 24 will take the door away for good. Here, keep the beep. If you remember nothing else, remember that the probe’s last second is not a second the ship is allowed to share.

---

\newpage

## 21. An Hour Beside the Edge

Clocks run slow when they sit deep in a gravitational well. This is not a rumor from a story. It is why a clock on a rear truck bed, driven up a mountain and back, comes home younger by a number you can write on a grant application. It is why GPS, without corrections, would slide your map into a river.

The first clean tower measurement has a name and a height. In 1959 and 1960, Robert Pound and Glen Rebka sent iron-57 gamma rays up and down a 22.5-meter shaft in Harvard’s Jefferson Laboratory. Einstein’s 1915 theory said the photons that climbed should arrive tired — a gravitational redshift of a couple of parts in 10¹⁵. The experiment was a nightmare of vibration and recoil, fixed with a trick of moving the source so the Doppler wobble could find the gravitational shift. They found it. The well is not a metaphor. It is a measurable tax on light.

In October 1971, Joseph Hafele and Richard Keating put cesium clocks on commercial airliners and flew them around the world, eastward and then westward, against twins that stayed at the United States Naval Observatory. Special relativity said speed would slow the fliers. General relativity said altitude would hurry them. East and west swapped the balance because the Earth is already spinning under the planes. The clocks disagreed when they met again, by hundreds of nanoseconds, in the direction the two effects predicted. The kitchen clock on the wall, the one that pretends to be the time of the cosmos, lost a small, public argument on a 747.

GPS lives on that argument. The satellites orbit at about 20,200 kilometers. Their clocks, left uncorrected, would run fast by roughly 45 microseconds a day from the weaker gravity and slow by about 7 microseconds a day from their speed. The net, about 38 microseconds a day, is 10 kilometers of map error if you ignore it. The phone in your pocket is a daily demonstration that a well changes a clock. You do not need a black hole to believe Chapter 5. You need a constellation and a ditch you would rather not drive into.

What “slow” means, if we are being careful, is not that the lower clock is broken. It is that its worldline is shorter between two meetings. Proper time is mileage. A path that sits deeper in a well is a shorter path, the way a trail that cuts through a valley can be shorter than a trail that stays on the ridge, except here the “shorter” is time itself. Pound and Rebka measured the tax on light. Hafele and Keating measured the tax on clocks that flew and clocks that stayed. GPS spends the tax every second so your map does not drown. The black-hole chapter is the same tax, made rude. Nothing new is invented at the horizon except the size of the bill.

![Figure 21. Two large clocks, one lower. The lower face is marked slow.](Figures/figs/fig21.png)

Near a horizon the same fact becomes rude. A clock hovering just outside, fighting with rockets to stay put, ticks slowly compared with a clock far away. An hour there can be years elsewhere. The numbers in posters — an hour for seven years — require a hole that is both enormous and spinning almost as fast as a hole is allowed to spin. Spin helps. A spinning hole, a Kerr hole, drags space with it and permits stable orbits closer in, where the well is deeper and the tides, for a given mass, are gentler. A supermassive hole can have an hour-beside-the-edge without spaghettifying the visitor. A stellar-mass hole would turn the visitor into a rumor of atoms before the hour was interesting.

Say the poster line out loud, then put a temperature on it. Warm claim: if you have a hole of millions or billions of Suns, spinning so close to the extremal limit that the leftover wiggle is a rounding error, and you park a station in the last orbits the equations still call stable, then an hour of proper time on the station can be years on a distant ship. Generic Kerr hole, generic orbit: the dilation is real and milder. Hours can be days. Days can be weeks. The seven-year postcard is a near-extremal special, not a brochure for every hole in the catalog. Appendix A21 writes the static factor *√(1 − r_s/r)* and notes how close to extremal spin the poster ratio really needs. Allowed is not the same as typical. Typical is not a holiday.

The innermost stable circular orbit — ISCO, in the jargon — is the last racetrack. For a hole that does not spin, it sits at three Schwarzschild radii. Matter in a disk can orbit outside that circle. Inside, it plunges. A Kerr hole, spinning, drags the loaf. Prograde orbits — going with the spin — can sit closer. In the extremal limit the last stable track crowds the horizon itself. That is why spin is not a flourish. It is how you get a station into a well deep enough for the poster, without asking the station to hover on rockets that would have to burn like a star. Even then the station is not comfortable. It is parked on a knife-edge the equations permit and astrophysics may not often build.

Frame-dragging is the grown-up name for the drag. A spinning mass pulls inertial frames around with it, the way a spoon pulled through honey pulls the honey. Lense and Thirring wrote the weak-field version a century ago; Gravity Probe B later measured a tiny twist of gyroscopes in Earth orbit and found the honey, faintly. Near a Kerr hole the honey is not faint. Orbits lean. Light leans. The last stable circle for a stone going with the spin can sit far closer than the last stable circle for a stone going against it. Accretion disks, the glowing junk that makes X-ray binaries and the EHT ring possible, live on those tracks. The woman in the station, if she exists at all, lives on one too. She is not hovering by brute force. She is falling sideways forever, on a rail the spin still allows. The rail is not a gift. Miss it inward and she plunges. Miss it outward and she is no longer in the poster well.

Tides are the other half of the honesty. The stretch across a body scales, near the membrane, as the inverse square of the hole’s mass. A ten-Sun hole, the sort LIGO hears, would pull your head from your feet before you had a philosophical thought about cones. Sagittarius A*, four million Suns, is gentler at its horizon; you could cross the membrane and still be a person for a while, which is not a kindness, only a delay. M87*, billions of Suns, has tides at the horizon that a human body would barely notice. The doom is the same. The itinerary of the doom is not. Do not confuse “the tides will not shred you at the door” with “the door is a place to live.”

Imagine a woman in a small station, parked in that close orbit, tending a rack of algae because someone still has to eat.

The station is the size of a bus that never learned to be a home. The algae live in clear sleeves along one wall, a green smear that bubbles when the pumps are honest. She checks the pH with a strip that stains her fingers. She eats a paste that was sunlight last month, then a smear, then a meal if you are not picky. The water tastes like the filter. The filter tastes like the last filter. There is a window, because the designer was sentimental, and the window shows a smear of lensed sky, a ring of light that is not for her. She does not go outside. Outside is a well.

Morning, by her watch, is a ritual with dirt on it. She bleeds a sleeve that has gone brown, dumps the dead green into a recycler that will pretend to be soil, and seeds a fresh rack because the oxygen budget is a number, not a vibe. The pumps click. A bubble sticks and she flicks the sleeve with a fingernail. This is the work. This is why she is here in the thought experiment: so the geometry has a person who still has to eat, not a tourist with a camera. The ship that sent her is a rumor with a call sign. The hole is not a view. The hole is a well she is parked in.

Her radio to a ship farther out is not just delayed by distance. It is delayed by *time*. She keys the mike and says “good morning” on a day her own log still calls Tuesday. The carrier crawls up the well. The ship hears it on a Thursday she will never have. The reply, when it comes, is already a conversation from a calendar she is not on. She is not time-traveling. She is accumulating less proper time between two meetings. Proper time is the mileage of her worldline, the number her watch is allowed to count. The ship’s worldline, higher on the same well, is longer between the same two reunions. The loaf did not rewind. Two worldlines of different lengths met again.

She keeps two logs because one log would lie. The station log is her proper time: sleep, pH, a paste that was algae, a Tuesday that belongs to her. The ship log, when the radio bothers to be kind, is a scatter of dates that skip. A maintenance window she was told would be “next week” arrives in her headphones as a report of a job already done. A birthday on the ship is a recording. She can answer the recording. She cannot attend the cake. “Now” is not a conference call. It is two mileages, and hers is the shorter.

She logs the algae. She sleeps. She wakes and the ship has had more hours than she has. If she stays a year by her count, the crew that sent her has had a different year — longer, grayer, full of Thursdays that were never hers. When she climbs back out, if the rockets and the orbits allow the climb, the ship’s crew has more gray in it than her mirror. She cannot take the extra years back. She can only have fewer of her own. This is the twin effect with a well instead of a rocket, and it is the only kind of “time travel to the future” relativity hands out for free. It is one-way in the human sense.

Do not book a ticket. This is not a resort and not a clinic. The station stinks of wet plastic. The radio is a stack of monologues, the way the greenhouse in Chapter 25 is a stack of monologues. The dilation that makes an hour into years is a near-extremal poster, not a civic plan. A person is a path, Chapter 5 said. A path that sits in a well is a shorter path. Shorter is not younger in the advertisement sense. Shorter is fewer of her seconds, spent on algae and a sky that does not belong to her. The book will not sell you a life beside a hole. It will sell you the geometry, and then it will send you back to the kitchen clock that was already lying.

Appendix A21 writes the dilation factor and the innermost stable orbit. Here, keep the two clocks. Keep the woman only long enough to feel that “now” was already in trouble in the kitchen. Beside the edge it is in ruins. She says good morning. Thursday answers. The casserole, if anyone still cares about casseroles, was done on a day she will read about and will not have lived.

---

\newpage

## 22. The Hole That Runs Backward

If you take a black hole and run the film of spacetime backward, you get a white hole: a membrane you can leave but never enter. Light can come out. Nothing from the outside can go in.
